\begin{frontmatter}

% \title{\magnus: neutrino oscillation probabilities for any standard and non-standard\\ Hermitian Hamiltonian, any number of flavors, any density profile}
\title{\magnus: neutrino oscillation probabilities for any Hermitian Hamiltonian,\\any number of flavors, and any matter profile}

\author{Mauricio Bustamante\corref{author}}
\ead{mbustamante@nbi.ku.dk}
\cortext[author]{ORCID: \href{http://orcid.org/0000-0001-6923-0865}{0000-0001-6923-0865}.}
\address{Niels Bohr International Academy, Niels Bohr Institute,\\University of Copenhagen, DK-2100 Copenhagen, Denmark}

\date{\today}

\begin{abstract}

Every measurement of neutrino oscillations rests on computing the probability that a neutrino born with one flavor is detected with a different one. In vacuum and constant-density matter, probabilities have exact formulas. Where the density varies along the path, as in the Sun, the Earth, or a supernova, they do not: numerical codes replace the profile by constant steps, converging slowly, or integrate the evolution step by step, paying for every oscillation. We present \magnus, an open-source Python code that computes them for any matter density profile, any number of flavors, and any physics written into the Hamiltonian: non-standard interactions, sterile neutrinos, Lorentz-invariance violation, pseudo-Dirac neutrinos, or a user's own model. \magnus\ is built to end the writing of a specialized solver for every experiment or scenario. The Magnus expansion solves the evolution of the neutrino state across a stretch where the Hamiltonian varies. The cost follows how the Hamiltonian varies, not how many oscillations the neutrino completes: the Sun, with hundreds of thousands, is as tractable as the Earth. There, \magnus\ switches by itself to transporting the state along the instantaneous eigenstates, keeping the Magnus expansion for the level crossings, where the evolution turns non-adiabatic and that transport fails. \magnus\ implements no approximate analytical formulas; its probabilities always add up to one and match independent checks to $10^{-12}$. It also returns the averaged probabilities solar and astrophysical neutrino measurements see. Ready-made cases include standard and non-standard scenarios spanning two to five flavors, with a full API, documentation, and tutorials. Against existing public codes, \magnus\ matches or beats most in speed and accuracy, with a flexibility few offer: a user's own Hamiltonian runs through the same call as the shipped ones.

% A neutrino created with one flavor can \textit{oscillate} and be detected later with another. Where the matter it crosses has constant density, the probability of this happening is a closed form. Where the density varies continuously---inside the Sun, through a supernova envelope, along a chord through the Earth---it is not, and the usual recourse is to replace the profile by a staircase of constant steps, or to integrate the evolution equation numerically. We take a third route. \magnus\ propagates the evolution operator with the \textit{Magnus expansion}: over each segment of the trajectory the operator is written as a single exponential whose argument is a truncated series of time-ordered integrals of the Hamiltonian, evaluated by Gauss--Legendre quadrature. Every truncation of that series remains in the Lie algebra, so the operator is unitary \emph{by construction} rather than to the accuracy of the truncation, at any order and any setting. The decisive practical consequence is that a segment is exact for a constant Hamiltonian however many oscillation cycles it spans: what sets its width is how fast the \emph{profile} changes, not how fast the \emph{phase} winds. A differential-equation solver has the opposite constraint, and pays for every radian. Nothing in the method fixes the size of the Hamiltonian or what it contains, so vacuum, matter, non-standard interactions, Lorentz-invariance violation, sterile states, five flavors, combinations of these, and a Hamiltonian nobody has written down yet are one and the same call. 


% \magnus\ also returns the phase-averaged, decohered probability---the quantity a solar experiment measures---in closed form, without propagating the $10^{6}$ radians that separate production from detection. We set out the method, the code, and a measured comparison against two other public codes, and we state where each of them wins.

\end{abstract}

\begin{keyword}

neutrino \sep oscillation \sep flavor \sep particle physics \sep Magnus expansion \sep matter effects

\end{keyword}

\end{frontmatter}

\begin{small}

\noindent
{\em Program title:} Mag$\nu$s ({\tt magnuspy}) v1.1.1 \\ \\
\smallskip
%%%%
{\em Licensing provisions:} GPL-3.0-only \\ \\
\smallskip
%%%%
{\em Program obtainable from:} \\
\href{https://github.com/mbustama/Magnus}{\tt https://github.com/mbustama/Magnus} \\
\href{https://pypi.org/project/magnuspy/}{\tt https://pypi.org/project/magnuspy/} \\
{\tt pip install magnuspy} \\ \\
\smallskip
%%%%
{\em Distribution format:} {\tt git} repository; source distribution and built wheel on the Python Package Index (PyPI), installable with {\tt pip} \\ \\
\smallskip
%%%%
{\em Programming language:} Python 3.10 or later; tested on 3.10, 3.11, 3.12, and 3.13 \\ \\
\smallskip
%%%%
{\em Classification:} 11.1 General, High Energy Physics and Computing \\ \\
\smallskip
%%%%
{\em External routines:} {\tt NumPy}, {\tt SciPy}, {\tt joblib}, {\tt Numba} and {\tt Matplotlib}, all installed automatically \\ \\
\smallskip
%%%%
{\em Nature of problem:} \\
Computation of neutrino flavor oscillation probabilities for an arbitrary number of flavors, in media whose density varies continuously along the trajectory, and for Hamiltonians that are time- or position-dependent --- including scenarios for which no closed form exists. \\ \\
\smallskip
%%%%
{\em Solution method:}\\
The evolution operator is propagated with the Magnus expansion. Truncated time-ordered integrals of the Hamiltonian are evaluated by Gauss--Legendre quadrature over a chain of position slabs and exponentiated; every truncation remains in the Lie algebra, so the operator is unitary by construction. Slab widths are chosen adaptively from a requested tolerance, and slab edges are placed on the discontinuities of a layered profile. Where the accumulated phase is extreme, an adiabatic transport is composed with exact local Magnus patches across the non-adiabatic windows. The phase-averaged, decohered limit is available in closed form, without propagating. \\ \\
\smallskip
%%%%
{\em Additional comments, including restrictions, unusual features:}\\
The Hamiltonian must be Hermitian and the evolution unitary: decay to invisible states, absorption, decoherence as an open-system process, and collective (self-interaction) effects lie outside the method and need a density-matrix or nonlinear solver instead. The requested tolerances {\tt rtol} and {\tt atol} are a \emph{stopping criterion, not an error bound}: refinement halts when two successive levels agree (evidence of convergence and not a certificate of it). The library documents this at length, warns where the evidence is weak, and reports the measured false-alarm rate of every warning it raises. Unusually for a code of this kind, the returned probability matrix is unitary to round-off at every accuracy setting, since each factor composed into the evolution operator is unitary by construction rather than by refinement; the residual defect is independent of the truncation order, the tolerance and the baseline, and grows only with the number of composed factors. \\ \\
%%%%
{\em Running time:}\\
Milliseconds per probability at the default tolerance; the median over 164 Earth and solar configurations is 2~ms. Whole scans over energy or arrival direction are batched, which is worth one to two orders of magnitude per probability over evaluating them one at a time.

\medskip

\end{small}

\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}

Neutrinos are created and detected in weak interactions as flavor states ($\nu_e$, $\nu_\mu$, $\nu_\tau$) but they propagate as superpositions of \emph{propagation states}, the eigenstates of $\mathbb{H}$, which in vacuum are the mass eigenstates\ \cite{Pontecorvo:1967fh, Maki:1962mu, Bilenky:2004sn, Fantini:2018itu}. Because the superposition evolves, a neutrino created with one flavor has a non-zero probability of being detected with another. The observation of that in solar, atmospheric, reactor, and accelerator neutrinos led to the discovery of neutrino mass and of flavor mixing in leptons\ \cite{Kajita:2016cak, McDonald:2016ixn}.

Computing the flavor-transition probability means solving the Schr\"{o}dinger equation
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:schrodinger}
 i\,\frac{d}{dl}\,\nu_\alpha(l) = \mathbb{H}(l)\,\nu_\alpha(l) \;,
\end{equation}
where $\nu_\alpha$ represents the flavor state of a neutrino born with flavor $\alpha = e$, $\mu$, $\tau$, $\mathbb{H}$ is the Hamiltonian in flavor space, and $l$ is the distance traveled by the neutrino since its creation along the trajectory, which for relativistic neutrinos---the predominant scenario---stands in for the elapsed time (\ie, $l \approx t)$. The probability of a $\nu_\alpha \to \nu_\beta$ transition is, therefore, $P_{\nu_\alpha \to \nu_\beta}(l) = \vert \nu_\beta^\dagger \nu_\alpha(l) \vert^2$.  Through $\mathbb{H}$, the probability is dependent on the neutrino energy. The probabilities are oscillatory in $l$; hence, flavor transitions are conventionally referred to as ``neutrino oscillations''.

The cost of computing flavor-transition probabilities reflects the complexity of $\mathbb{H}$, itself an encoding of the environment in which the neutrino propagates and the physics it is subject to. Where $\mathbb{H}$ does not depend on $l$, the solution of \equ{schrodinger} is simply $\nu_\alpha(l) = e^{-i \mathbb{H} l}\,\nu_\alpha(0)$---with $\mathbb{U} \equiv e^{-i \mathbb{H} l}$ the evolution operator---and the problem is closed: the oscillation probabilities in vacuum and in matter of constant density have exact solutions. The companion code {\tt NuOscProbExact}\ \cite{NuOscProbExact, Bustamante:2019ggq} evaluates them for an arbitrary Hamiltonian at two, three, and four flavors. This covers important scenarios such as the propagation of astrophysical neutrinos in the vacuum of outer space and of natural and human-made neutrinos in the Earth's crust.

One quantity governs everything that follows. The \emph{accumulated phase} along a trajectory is
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:phase}
 \Phi
 \equiv
 \int_{l_0}^{l_1} \left[\lambda_{\rm max}(l) - \lambda_{\rm min}(l)\right] dl \;,
\end{equation}
with $\lambda_{\rm max}$ and $\lambda_{\rm min}$ the largest and smallest instantaneous eigenvalues of $\mathbb{H}(l)$. It is the total phase difference the fastest-separating pair of propagation states builds up between production and detection, measured in radians. It is what any method that follows the oscillation must resolve. In vacuum at three flavors it reduces, \eg, to the familiar $\Delta m^2_{31} L / 2E$, where $\Delta m^2_{31} \equiv m_3^2 - m_1^2$ is the difference in the masses of neutrino mass eigenstates $\nu_3$ and $\nu_1$ (which are the propagation states in this case), $L$ is the total path length, and $E$ is the neutrino energy. For a 1-GeV neutrino crossing the Earth along a diameter, $\Phi$ is some $80$ rad; for a 5-MeV neutrino leaving the Sun, some $9 \cdot 10^{5}$ rad (and $2.6 \cdot 10^{4}$ rad on the slower pair, $\nu_2$ and $\nu_1$, which is the one the solar survival probability answers to); and for a TeV neutrino from $100$~Mpc, some $2 \cdot 10^{16}$ rad.

The difficulty this paper addresses is where the Hamiltonian varies \emph{continuously} along the path. The more common manifestation of this is the propagation of neutrinos in matter of continuously varying density. Then $\mathbb{H}(l)$ does not commute with itself at different positions and the evolution operator is not simply the exponential of the Hamiltonian. This leaves two standard recourses. One is to replace the matter profile by a staircase: cut the path into slabs of constant density, solve each in closed form, and compose the operators in order. The other is to integrate \equ{schrodinger} numerically with a differential-equation solver. Each has a characteristic failure. The staircase converges only at second order in the slab width, the rate of the midpoint rule it amounts to (Section \ref{sec:order_is_the_slab_product}). An adaptive solver must instead resolve the oscillation phase step by step, so its cost grows with $\Phi$. Neither is about the physics in the Hamiltonian; both are about the shape of the profile and the size of $\Phi$.

 \caption{\label{fig:validation}Survival probability $P_{\nu_e \to \nu_e}$ at two, three, four, and five flavors. {\it Top}: the probability along one exponential profile of central density $3 \cdot 10^{3}$~g~cm$^{-3}$ and scale height $10$~km over a $25$~km baseline, computed with \magnus\ \cite{Magnus}. Only the energy window differs between panels, since an eV-scale $\Delta m^2_{41}$ and a Standard-Model splitting do not resonate at the same energy. {\it Bottom}: the deviation from a {\tt DOP853} integration of the \emph{same} Hamiltonian, so that no convention can differ between the two; the departure of the probability matrix from row-normalization; and the reference's own convergence spread, obtained by tightening it from ${\tt rtol} = 10^{-12}$ to $10^{-13}$---the curve the panel labels the \emph{oracle floor}, the oracle being whatever independent calculation a quantity is being scored against, here the {\tt DOP853} integration. Every figure in this paper comes from notebook \#28 (\ref{app:notebooks})}
\end{figure*}

\section{Synopsis}
\label{sec:synopsis}

To address this, we introduce \magnus\ \cite{Magnus}\footnote{Install via {\tt pip install magnuspy}\;.  Tutorials at \Ref~\cite{MagnusDocs}.}, a code that returns oscillation probabilities by computing the evolution operator using the \textit{Magnus expansion}\ \cite{Magnus:1954zz, Blanes:2008xlr}. Over a segment of the trajectory of the neutrino---within which the Hamiltonian may be varying---the expansion allows the evolution operator to be written as a single exponential, $\mathbb{U} = \exp(\Omega)$, where $\Omega$ is a series of 
% time-ordered 
integrals of nested commutators of the Hamiltonian. 

What that form buys is three advantages, independently of what the Hamiltonian contains: robustness, speed, and flexibility. \magnus\ is \emph{robust}: because the evolution operator is exactly unitary at any order, any tolerance, and any slab count, the probabilities always add up to one, within numerical round-off. \magnus~is \emph{fast where the phase is large}: cost follows the profile and not the phase, where a differential-equation solver pays for every radian. And \magnus~is \emph{flexible}: the Hamiltonian is a callable returning a Hermitian matrix of any size, so five flavors, non-standard interactions, a Lorentz-violating background, and a completely novel neutrino interaction are one and the same call. Sections \ref{sec:method} and \ref{sec:performance} measure each of them.

Figure~\ref{fig:validation} shows the survival probability $P_{\nu_e \to \nu_e}$ of a neutrino crossing an exponentially falling density profile, computed with \magnus\ at two, three, four, and five flavors, the last two representing non-standard oscillations with one and two sterile neutrino states. The deviation from an independent integration of the same Hamiltonian is $10^{-12}$ or smaller.
% , which is the accuracy of that integration itself, and the probabilities sum to one to round-off. 

Listing~\ref{lst:validation} shows that generating the data for \figu{validation} is only a few lines of code. The listing calls four of the wrappers \magnus\ ships for the cases most often met: one per flavor count, differing only in the energy window and in the mixing parameters that count adds. The wrappers are a provided as a convenience to the user: each builds a Hamiltonian internally and hands it to the core function of \magnus, which takes an arbitrary Hermitian matrix of any size. Nothing in the method fixes the number of flavors, the form of the Hamiltonian, or the physics inside it, so a scenario \magnus\ ships no wrapper for is computed by the same call with a different matrix. Section~\ref{sec:examples} explains the usage.

While the Magnus expansion is at the core of \magnus, the code does not rely on the expansion alone. Where the accumulated phase is extreme and the profile varies slowly, as in the Sun, \magnus\ transports the state along the instantaneous eigenstates of $\mathbb{H}$ and reserves the expansion for the narrow windows in which two eigenvalues approach and that transport fails. \magnus\ decide the hand-over from the Hamiltonian itself, with no intervention from the user (Sec.~\ref{sec:hybrid}).

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python, caption={Computing the data in \figu{validation} with \magnus: the $\nu_e$ survival probability along an exponentially falling density profile at two, three, four (3+1), and five (3+2) flavors (left to right). One wrapper serves each flavor count; the four calls differ only in the energy window and in the mixing each count adds. The oscillation parameters are best-fit values from the NuFIT 6.1 global oscillation fit~\cite{Esteban:2024eli} (normal ordering, with Super-Kamiokande atmospheric data). The only nonzero active-sterile mixing parameters are $\sin \theta_{14} = \sin \theta_{24} = \sqrt{0.1}$, $\Delta m_{41}^2 = 1.0$~eV$^2$ in the 3+1 case; and $\sin \theta_{15} = \sin \theta_{25} = \sqrt{0.06}$, $\Delta m_{41}^2 = 1.0$~eV$^2$, $\Delta m_{51}^2 = 1.7$~eV$^2$ in the 3+2 case.}, label={lst:validation}, float=t]
import numpy as np
import magnus.globaldefs as gd
import magnus.oscprob as oscprob

# Standard oscillation parameters
osc = gd.load_nufit_params('NuFIT 6.1')
# Active-sterile mixing
s14 = s24 = np.sqrt(0.10)
s15 = s25 = np.sqrt(0.06)

def energies(lo, hi):
    return np.logspace(np.log10(lo), np.log10(hi),
                       140)*gd.UNIT_GEV

# Exponentially falling density: 3e3 g/cm^3 at the
# origin, scale height 10 km, 25 km baseline.
# Every mixing parameter left out defaults to zero.
common = dict(L=25.0*gd.UNIT_KM, L0=0.0,
              rho_central=3.e3,
              l_scale=10.0*gd.UNIT_KM,
              density_matter_is_in_g_per_cm3=True,
              nu_i=gd.NUE, nu_f=gd.NUE,
              rtol=1e-12, atol=1e-14)

P_2nu = oscprob.osc_prob_2nu_matter_exp_density(
    energies(0.0005, 0.05), **common,
    sth=osc['s12'], Dm2=osc['D21'])

P_3nu = oscprob.osc_prob_3nu_matter_exp_density(
    energies(0.002, 0.2), **common, **osc)

P_4nu = oscprob.osc_prob_4nu_matter_exp_density(
    energies(2.0, 20.0), **common, **osc,
    s14=s14, s24=s24, D41=1.0)

P_5nu = oscprob.osc_prob_5nu_matter_exp_density(
    energies(2.0, 20.0), **common, **osc,
    s14=s14, s15=s15, s24=s24, s25=s25,
    D41=1.0, D51=1.7)
\end{lstlisting}

\magnus~ships with Hamiltonians that tackle commonly explored scenarios---including propagation in vacuum, in uniform matter, inside the Earth and the Sun, through a supernova shock---and using both standard and new-physics cases. The latter includes, but is not limited to, non-standard neutrino-matter interactions, Lorentz-invariance violation, sterile neutrinos, and pseudo-Dirac neutrinos. \magnus~ships these cases readily available for up to five neutrino flavors (\ie, three active flavors, plus two additional ones), but is easily extendable to more. Further, \magnus~also returns the \emph{phase-averaged} (decohered) form of the probabilities, \ie, the $L/E \to \infty$ limit a solar or astrophysical measurement is sensitive to because it integrates over many cycles of a phase that cannot be resolved. Finally, \magnus ~ships optimized functions to compute and render commonly used plots in the field, such as of probability vs.~distance and vs.~energy, oscillograms, and bi-probability plots.  The application programming interface (API) is extensively documented and made publicly available, along with implementation details, and a large number of tutorial notebooks.

% We present the method in full later. For now, two properties follow.

% First, \textbf{truncating the series preserves total probability (\ie, the sum of all probabilities is unity) even if it yields an approximate answer}. Each term of $\Omega$ is a real combination of nested commutators of anti-Hermitian matrices and is therefore itself anti-Hermitian, so any truncation stays in the Lie algebra and its exponential is \emph{exactly} unitary. Probabilities are non-negative and their rows sum to one (to numerical round-off at implementation) at any truncation order, any quadrature accuracy, and any slab count. What that buys is not exactness but independence: the residual defect does not respond to the accuracy dials at all, growing only with the number of factors composed. Figure~\ref{fig:validation} shows this beside the accuracy. The unitarity defect and the accuracy live at different scales; the former does not move even when the latter does.

% Second, \textbf{a segment is exact for a constant Hamiltonian however many oscillation cycles it spans}. When $\mathbb{H}$ does not vary, every nested commutator vanishes, the series terminates at its first term, and $\exp(-i\mathbb{H}\Delta l)$ is not an approximation to be refined but the answer. What forces a segment to be narrow is therefore the variation of the \emph{profile} across it, not the phase accumulated inside it. A differential-equation solver has the opposite constraint. Section~\ref{sec:phase_vs_profile} measures both statements.

% Three further properties follow from the same construction and are not shared by the closed forms. There is no ceiling on the number of flavors. The SU($n$) constructions that give closed-form probabilities need the eigenvalues of the Hamiltonian in radicals, which the Abel-Ruffini theorem forbids past four~\cite{Bustamante:2019ggq}. 
% There is no ceiling on the number of flavors. Nothing in the Magnus expansion refers to the size of the matrix at all; we ship two, three, four, and five flavors with the code, but there is no ceiling on what the Hamiltonian contains. In the code, it is a callable returning a Hermitian matrix, so non-standard interactions, a Lorentz-violating background, sterile states (or all of them at once), or a completely novel term are the same call with a different matrix. In addition, \texttt{Magnus} also returns the \emph{phase-averaged} (decohered) limit of the probabilities, \ie, the $L/E \to \infty$ limit a solar or astrophysical measurement returns because it integrates over many cycles of a phase that cannot be resolved, computed in closed form from the eigenvectors of $\mathbb{H}$ rather than by propagating that phase.


Section \ref{sec:recap} fixes notation. Section \ref{sec:method} presents the method. Section \ref{sec:code} describes the code, Section \ref{sec:examples} shows it in use, Section \ref{sec:performance} measures its speed and precision, and Section \ref{sec:comparison} compares it against two other public codes on problems refereed by a third method. Section \ref{sec:conclusions} concludes; the appendices hold supporting detail: the structure of the expansion, the test suite, the notebooks, and the conventions behind the Earth comparison.

% \section{Overview and context}
% \label{sec:scope}

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

% \subsection{Overview of the method}
% \label{sec:overview}

% The Magnus expansion is due to Magnus\ \cite{Magnus:1954zz} and has a large literature in numerical analysis, where it is one of the standard geometric integrators---methods that preserve a structure of the problem exactly rather than to within the truncation error\ \cite{Iserles:2000acta, Hairer:2006gni}. \Ref\ \cite{Blanes:2008xlr} reviews it, and \Ref\ \cite{Blanes:2000bit} derives the Gauss--Legendre \emph{collocation} integrators that \magnus\ uses by default---collocation meaning that the Hamiltonian is sampled at a few chosen points inside each step, here the Gauss--Legendre nodes, and the scheme built so that those samples deliver the full order.

% The same expansion is known in magnetic resonance as average Hamiltonian theory, where it arrived in 1968\ \cite{Waugh:1968, Haeberlen:1968zz, Evans:1968} and became a design discipline rather than only a series: pulse sequences are chosen so that named orders of the expansion vanish identically, with symmetry arguments canceling whole classes of higher-order terms\ \cite{Haeberlen:1976}. What we provide is its implementation for neutrino flavor evolution: the expansion carried to order 10, the quadratures, the slab chain and its adaptive refinement, an exactly unitary matrix exponential, the composition with adiabatic transport for extreme accumulated phase, the closed-form decohered limit, and the engineering that makes a whole scan over energy or arrival direction one batched call rather than thousands of separate ones.

% The expansion has been used for neutrino oscillations before. It was brought to two-flavor evolution in matter by D'Olivo and Oteo\ \cite{DOlivo:1990xs}, developed for realistic profiles by Ioannisian and Smirnov\ \cite{Ioannisian:2008ve}, and turned into a practical integrator for the three-flavor problem by Casas \emph{et al.}\ \cite{Casas:2016asi}, who report gains of up to two orders of magnitude in speed over general-purpose differential-equation solvers. What has not been available is a general-purpose, tested, documented implementation. It takes an arbitrary Hermitian Hamiltonian at an arbitrary number of flavors, chooses its own discretization from a requested tolerance, and places its slab edges on the discontinuities of a layered profile. It also says which of its six internal engines answered a call---the independent methods that can serve a request, listed in Section \ref{sec:engines}---and reports, rather than assuming, when the evidence for its own convergence is weak, naming how far from converged it stopped.

% Two limits bound everything that follows. The Hamiltonian must be Hermitian, which is what makes the evolution unitary and the system closed; and the Magnus series is guaranteed to converge absolutely while $\int \lVert \mathbb{H} \rVert_2\, dl < \pi$ over the interval the expansion is applied to\ \cite{Moan:2008conv}. Here $\lVert \cdot \rVert_2$ is the spectral norm, the largest singular value, which for a Hermitian matrix is the largest eigenvalue in modulus. The condition is sufficient rather than necessary---a particular $\mathbb{H}$ may converge well beyond it---but it is sharp in the sense that a counterexample exists at $\pi$, so it is the bound to design slabs against. The first is a restriction on the physics and Section \ref{sec:limitations} says what it excludes. The second is not, because the interval is the code's to choose: a long trajectory is cut into as many slabs as the criterion needs. \magnus\ warns when a slab looks unsafe, testing $\lVert \Omega \rVert_2 \ge \pi$ rather than the integral itself. Since $\lVert \Omega \rVert_2 \le \int \lVert \mathbb{H} \rVert_2\,dl$, that is a lower bound on the quantity in the criterion. The test therefore under-reports. A slab it complains about is genuinely outside the bound, so there are no false alarms; a slab it passes may still be outside, so silence is not a guarantee. It is cheap, and it is the conservative half of the check rather than the whole of it.

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

% \subsection{Relation to other oscillation codes}
% \label{sec:relation}

% Several mature codes compute oscillation probabilities. They overlap less than their common subject suggests. {\tt GLoBES}\ \cite{GLoBES, Huber:2007ji} is an experiment-simulation framework in which probabilities are one layer inside a package built for fluxes, cross-sections and detector response. {\tt Prob3++}\ \cite{Prob3pp} evaluates the analytic three-flavor probability in matter\ \cite{Barger:1980tf} and is long established in atmospheric analyses. {\tt nuCraft}\ \cite{nuCraft} integrates numerically along a chord through the Earth. {\tt NuFast-LBL}\ \cite{Denton:2024pzc} and {\tt NuFast-Earth}\ \cite{Denton:2025oxf} are fast specializations of the three-flavor case. {\tt NuOscProbExact}\ \cite{NuOscProbExact, Bustamante:2019ggq} evaluates closed-form SU($n$) expansions of the evolution operator for an arbitrary Hermitian Hamiltonian at two, three, and four flavors, and composes them across slabs of constant density. {\tt nuSQuIDS}\ \cite{Delgado:2014kpa, Arguelles:2021twb, NuSQuIDS} is the most general: it propagates a density matrix rather than a state, which admits interactions, attenuation and open-system terms that no closed form reaches, at the cost of solving a differential equation rather than composing exponentials.

% Several others serve particular sectors: {\tt OscProb}\ \cite{OscProb} and {\tt CUDAProb3}\ \cite{Kallenborn:2019ilo} evaluate layered Earth profiles for atmospheric analyses, the latter on graphics hardware; {\tt Neurthino.jl}\ \cite{Neurthino} and {\tt NuProbe}\ \cite{Fong:2022oim} extend beyond three flavors; {\tt Nu-Pert}\ \cite{NuPert} expands about a constant density; and {\tt PEANUTS}\ \cite{PEANUTS} computes the solar flux and its composition on an adiabatic treatment, which makes it the natural comparison for the averaged solar observable of Section \ref{sec:sun}. Three of these codes now return derivatives of the probability---analytically in {\tt CHIC}\ \cite{Fernandez-Menendez:2025qbi}, by automatic differentiation in {\tt MANGO}\ \cite{Granger:2026qdr} and {\tt nuTens}\ \cite{nuTens}---which \magnus\ does not, and which a user fitting parameters will want to know before adopting it.

% \magnus\ sits between the last two. The difference from each is structural rather than a matter of degree. Against a closed-form slab code, the difference is what happens \emph{inside} a slab: {\tt NuOscProbExact} freezes the Hamiltonian there and is exact for the frozen one, while \magnus\ integrates the Hamiltonian across the slab and is exact to the order of its truncation for the real one. Section \ref{sec:order_is_the_slab_product} shows that the two coincide exactly at second order---a piecewise-constant composition with the density read at each slab's midpoint \emph{is} the order-2 Magnus method---so what we add on the same slab chain is the higher orders, and the flavor counts and Hamiltonians that a closed form cannot express. Against {\tt nuSQuIDS}, the difference is that a unitary state-vector method cannot represent an open system at all, and pays nothing for the generality it does not have.

% Section \ref{sec:comparison} measures \magnus\ against {\tt NuOscProbExact} and {\tt nuSQuIDS} directly, with every case refereed by a method that is neither code's, and states where each of the three wins.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Neutrino oscillations: notation and conventions}
\label{sec:recap}

In a system of $n \geq 2$ neutrino flavors, $\nu_\alpha$ is the flavor state of a neutrino, represented by an $n$-component column vector, and $\mathbb{H}$ is the $n \times n$ Hamiltonian in flavor space. We use natural units, $c = \hbar = 1$, with energies in eV and distances in eV$^{-1}$, so that $\mathbb{H}L$ is dimensionless. Every length crossing the package's interface is in eV$^{-1}$: baselines, radii, slab edges, breakpoints alike. The factor {\tt globaldefs.UNIT\_KM} converts kilometers into eV$^{-1}$---multiply by it---one kilometer being some $5.07 \cdot 10^{9}$~eV$^{-1}$. This is the one convention worth stating as a rule rather than meeting case by case, because getting it wrong does not raise errors: a baseline handed over in kilometers is a chord a few millimeters long, and the call returns a converged, unitary probability for it. \magnus\ warns when a length is small enough to be kilometers left unconverted, but does not halt.

Because neutrinos are relativistic, we identify the elapsed time with the distance traveled and write both as $l$. The state obeys \equ{schrodinger}; its solution defines the evolution operator $\mathbb{U}(l_1, l_0)$  through $\nu_\alpha(l_1) = \mathbb{U}(l_1,l_0)\, \nu_
\alpha(l_0)$, with $\mathbb{U}(l_0,l_0) = \mathbb{1}$. The probability that a neutrino born as $\nu_\alpha$ is detected as $\nu_\beta$ is
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:probability}
 P_{\nu_\alpha \to \nu_\beta}(l_1, l_0)
 =
 \left\lvert \left[\mathbb{U}(l_1,l_0)\right]_{\beta\alpha} \right\rvert^2 \;.
\end{equation}
We take $\mathbb{H}$ Hermitian throughout; $\mathbb{U}$ is then unitary and $\sum_\beta P_{\nu_\alpha \to \nu_\beta} = 1$ for every $\alpha$. Flavor indices run over $e, \mu, \tau$ at $n=3$ and over $e, \mu, \tau, s_1$ at $n = 4$ and $e, \mu, \tau, s_1, s_2$ at $n = 5$, the states $s_i$ being sterile, which interact only through their mixing.

As an example, in vacuum, the Hamiltonian is
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:h_vacuum}
 \mathbb{H}^{\rm vac}(E)
 =
 \frac{1}{2E}\, \mathbb{R}\, \mathbb{M}^2\, \mathbb{R}^\dagger \;,
\end{equation}
where $\mathbb{M}^2 = {\rm diag}(0, \Delta m^2_{21}, \Delta m^2_{31}, \ldots)$, $\Delta m^2_{ij} \equiv m_i^2 - m_j^2$, $m_i$ and $m_j$ are the masses of the neutrino mass eigenstates $\nu_i$ and $\nu_j$, and $\mathbb{R}$ is the mixing matrix that rotates between the flavor and mass bases.  The matrix is parametrized by the rotation angles and CP-violation phases. At three neutrinos---the standard case---it corresponds to the Pontecorvo-Maki-Nakagawa-Sakata (PMNS) mixing matrix, parametrized in terms of three mixing angles, $\theta_{12}$, $\theta_{23}$, and $\theta_{13}$, and one phase, $\delta_{\rm CP}$. 

In matter, neutrinos experience coherent forward scattering on matter via charged-current (CC) interactions. As a result, the Hamiltonian acquires the Mikheev-Smirnov-Wolfenstein (MSW) term\ \cite{Wolfenstein:1977ue, Mikheev:1986gs}
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:h_matter}
 \mathbb{H}(E, l)
 =
 \mathbb{H}^{\rm vac}(E)
 +
 V_{\rm CC}(l)\, \mathbb{P} \;,
 \quad
 V_{\rm CC} = \sqrt{2}\, G_F\, n_e(l) \;,
\end{equation}
where $V_{\rm CC}$ is the matter potential, $n_e$ is the electron number density, $G_F$ is the Fermi constant, and $\mathbb{P}$, the projector, is a fixed matrix. The projector is where the flavor count enters. 

At three flavors, $\mathbb{P} = {\rm diag}(1,0,0)$: the neutral-current (NC) potential is common to the three active flavors and cancels from the probabilities. With a sterile state, it no longer cancels, since a sterile neutrino is a gauge singlet and feels neither potential. In this case, $\mathbb{P} = {\rm diag}(1, 0, 0, r/2, \ldots)$ with $r = n_n/n_p$ the ratio of neutrons to protons. The $r/2$ factor is explained as follows. The NC potential of an active flavor is $V_{\rm NC} = -G_F n_n/\sqrt{2}$. Subtracting it from the whole diagonal is what removes it from the active flavors; what it leaves on a sterile state is $-V_{\rm NC}$. With $n_p = n_e$ by charge neutrality, $-V_{\rm NC} = G_F n_n/\sqrt{2} = (n_n/n_p)\,G_F n_e/\sqrt{2} = (r/2)\,V_{\rm CC}$. So the sterile entry is not an interaction of the sterile state but the residue of the subtraction, and it is $1/2$ for isoscalar matter (\ie, matter with $n_n = n_p$). \magnus\ builds $\mathbb{P}$ in {\tt matter.matter\_potential\_projector}, for every flavor count. (\magnus\ is not limited to isoscalar matter, though; the user can feed their own Hamiltonian for non-isoscalar matter to the solver.)

For antineutrinos, the mixing matrix is conjugated \emph{and} the matter potential changes sign. The second operation is applied once, inside the potential constructor, and doing only one of the two returns a plausible, but wrong answer\ \cite{MagnusDocs}. To prevent this from happening, shipped functions include a \texttt{nubar} flag that, when activated, performs both operations.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The method}
\label{sec:method}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{History and context}

The Magnus expansion is due to Magnus\ \cite{Magnus:1954zz} and has a large literature in numerical analysis, where it is one of the standard geometric integrators---methods that preserve a structure of the problem exactly rather than to within the truncation error\ \cite{Iserles:2000acta, Hairer:2006gni}. Reference~\cite{Blanes:2008xlr} reviews it, and \Ref\ \cite{Blanes:2000bit} derives the Gauss-Legendre \emph{collocation} integrators that \magnus\ uses by default---collocation meaning that the Hamiltonian is sampled at a few chosen points inside each step, here Gauss-Legendre nodes, and the scheme is built so that those samples deliver the full order. 

The expansion has been used for neutrino oscillations before. It was brought to two-flavor evolution in matter by D'Olivo and Oteo\ \cite{DOlivo:1990xs}, developed for realistic profiles by Ioannisian and Smirnov\ \cite{Ioannisian:2008ve}, and turned into a practical integrator for the three-flavor problem by Casas \emph{et al.}\ \cite{Casas:2016asi}, who reported gains of up to two orders of magnitude in speed over general-purpose differential-equation solvers. 

What has not been available is a general-purpose, tested, documented implementation, which \magnus\ is. It takes an arbitrary Hermitian Hamiltonian at an arbitrary number of flavors, chooses its own discretization from a requested tolerance, and places its slab edges on the discontinuities of a layered profile. It also says which of its six internal engines answered a call---the independent methods that can serve a request, listed in Section \ref{sec:engines}---and reports, rather than assuming, when the evidence for its own convergence is weak, naming how far from converged it stopped.  \magnus\ implements the Magnus expansion carried to order 10 (the maximum by default, extendable), the quadratures, the slab chain and its adaptive refinement, an exactly unitary matrix exponential, the composition with adiabatic transport for extreme accumulated phase, the closed-form decohered limit, and the engineering that makes a whole scan over neutrino energy or arrival direction one batched call rather than thousands of separate ones.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{Where the method applies and where it does not}
\label{sec:applicability}

The method, and its implementation in \magnus, asks for one thing only: \textbf{that the Hamiltonian be Hermitian}. That is what makes the evolution unitary and the system closed, so that the total number of neutrinos, summed across all flavors, is constant. Section \ref{sec:limitations} says what cases this requirement excludes; they include different forms of open systems, like invisible neutrino decay and decoherence. In those cases, other codes should be used, like \texttt{nuSQuIDS}~\cite{Arguelles:2021twb, NuSQuIDS}.

Convergence of the Magnus expansion is not a second requirement but a constraint on the slab width. It is the code that internally meets the requirement, not the user. The series, which we introduce formally in Sec.~\ref{sec:expansion}, converges absolutely provided $\int \lVert \mathbb{H} \rVert_2\, dl < \pi$ over the interval the expansion is applied to\ \cite{Moan:2008conv}, where $\lVert \cdot \rVert_2$ is the spectral norm; for a Hermitian matrix, it is the largest eigenvalue in modulus. The condition is sufficient rather than necessary, so a particular $\mathbb{H}$ may converge well beyond it. But $\pi$ cannot be replaced by anything larger: there is a Hamiltonian whose series diverges as soon as the integral reaches it. Therefore, it is the bound to choose slabs against. For a given tolerance, \magnus\ chooses the position and width of slabs: a long trajectory is cut into as many as the criterion needs, without user intervention. (The caller may also call \magnus\ with a specific number of slabs rather than a requested tolerance.) Whenever the code fails to reach a requested tolerance---because, \eg, the cap on the slab count was set too low---the code reports so.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{The Magnus expansion}
\label{sec:expansion}
Writing $\mathbb{A}(l) \equiv -i\,\mathbb{H}(l)$, \equ{schrodinger} reads $d\mathbb{U}/dl = \mathbb{A}(l)\,\mathbb{U}$. Where $\mathbb{A}$ commutes with itself at different positions (\ie, for neutrinos, in vacuum, uniform density), the solution is $\exp \left( \int \mathbb{A}\,dl \right)$; where it does not, that expression is wrong. The time-ordered exponential that replaces it is the Magnus expansion\ \cite{Magnus:1954zz}:
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:magnus}
 \mathbb{U}(l, l_0) = \exp\left[\Omega(l, l_0)\right] \;,
 \qquad
 \Omega = \sum_{k \geq 1} \Omega_k \;,
\end{equation}
\emph{exactly}, with the terms generated by a recursion in the Bernoulli numbers $B_j$ (in the $B_1 = -1/2$ convention)\ \cite{Blanes:2008xlr},
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:recursion}
 \Omega_1 = \int_{l_0}^{l} \mathbb{A}\,ds \;,
 \quad
 \Omega_k = \sum_{j=1}^{k-1} \frac{B_j}{j!} \int_{l_0}^{l} S_k^{(j)}\, ds \;,
\end{equation}
where $S_k^{(1)} = [\Omega_{k-1}, \mathbb{A}]$ and $S_k^{(j)} = \sum_{m=1}^{k-j} [\Omega_m, S_{k-m}^{(j-1)}]$. The time-ordering means that the earlier-time operators are applied first, \ie, are positioned to the right of later-time operators.

The first three terms read
\begin{align}
 \label{equ:omega123}
 \Omega_1 &= \int_{l_0}^{l}\! ds\, \mathbb{A}(s) \;, \nonumber \\
 \Omega_2 &= \frac{1}{2}\int_{l_0}^{l}\! ds_1 \int_{l_0}^{s_1}\! ds_2\,
 \left[\mathbb{A}(s_1), \mathbb{A}(s_2)\right] \;, \\
 \Omega_3 &= \frac{1}{6}\int_{l_0}^{l}\! ds_1 \int_{l_0}^{s_1}\! ds_2
 \int_{l_0}^{s_2}\! ds_3 \Big(
 \left[\mathbb{A}_1, [\mathbb{A}_2, \mathbb{A}_3]\right] \nonumber \\
 &\hspace{3.1cm} + \left[\mathbb{A}_3, [\mathbb{A}_2, \mathbb{A}_1]\right]
 \Big) \;, \nonumber
\end{align}
with $\mathbb{A}_i \equiv \mathbb{A}(s_i)$. \ref{app:terms} gives the structure at any order. The odd Bernoulli numbers vanish for $j \geq 3$, so whole commutator groups drop out and only $j = 1, 2, 4, 6, \ldots$ contribute. Every term unrolls to a right-nested chain $[\Omega_{m_1}, [\Omega_{m_2}, \ldots [\Omega_{m_j}, \mathbb{A}]\ldots]]$ with $\sum_i m_i = k-1$, so the terms of the $j$-th group are indexed by the compositions of $k-1$ into $j$ positive parts and there are $\binom{k-2}{j-1}$ of them. Their number roughly doubles per order: $\Omega_7$ has $17$ terms and $\Omega_{10}$ has $129$.

In neutrino oscillations, the first term of the Magnus expansion is the one driving the standard slab approximation. Where $\mathbb{H}$ does not depend on position, $\Omega_1 = -i\mathbb{H}\Delta l$ is the whole of the expansion, since every higher term is a nested commutator of $\mathbb{H}$ with itself and vanishes identically. Its exponential is then the exact evolution operator, not a truncation of one. Evaluating it is exactly the problem {\tt NuOscProbExact}\ \cite{NuOscProbExact, Bustamante:2019ggq} solves in closed form: expanding $\mathbb{H}$ and $e^{-i\mathbb{H}L}$ in the generators of SU($n$) returns the probability from traces alone, without ever constructing an eigenvector, and exactly to round-off at every flavor count for which the eigenvalues can be written down. Composing one such operator per slab, in the order the neutrino meets them, is the standard slab treatment of a piecewise-constant profile. We take that correspondence further in Section \ref{sec:order_is_the_slab_product}.
% , where the correspondence is an identity rather than an analogy: reading the density at each slab's midpoint makes the slab composition the order-2 Magnus method, term for term.

\magnus\ implements \equ{recursion} through order 10. The first six orders are hard-coded inline in the simplified forms from \Ref\ \cite{Blanes:2008xlr}. Beyond that ($k \geq 7$), terms are generated from the recursion.  A separate module, {\tt expansionterms}, derives the same terms symbolically in exact rational arithmetic at \emph{any} order and shares no code with the numerical core. The two implementations of the Magnus expansion agree, evidencing correctness. (Users interested in applying the Magnus expansion to other problems---in magnetic resonance, quantum optics, lattice gauge theory, or condensed matter---may repurpose this module.) \ref{app:tests} states that the test suite regenerates the hard-coded recursion coefficients and compares the two.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{Truncation is exactly unitary}
\label{sec:unitarity}

Because $\mathbb{H}$ is  Hermitian, $\mathbb{A} = -i\mathbb{H}$ is anti-Hermitian. The commutator of two anti-Hermitian matrices is again anti-Hermitian. Every $\Omega_k$ in \equ{recursion} is a real-coefficient combination of nested commutators of $\mathbb{A}$ with itself, so every $\Omega_k$ is anti-Hermitian, as is any partial sum. Therefore
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:unitary}
 \Omega^\dagger = -\Omega
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \left[\exp(\Omega)\right]^\dagger \exp(\Omega) = \mathbb{1}
\end{equation}
for \emph{every} truncation order and every quadrature accuracy. Truncating early costs accuracy; it never costs unitarity. This is the sense in which the method is a geometric integrator: the exact solution lies in the unitary group. Truncating the series and evaluating its integrals numerically are both approximations, and neither leaves the group: $\exp(\Omega)$ is in it at any setting, rather than merely closing on it as the discretization is refined.

In practice, the guarantee of unitarity is only as good as the exponential that realizes it: a computed operator inherits it only if the routine that exponentiates $\Omega$ preserves it. A general-purpose Pad\'e routine does not: it knows nothing of the structure of its argument and returns a matrix that is unitary only to its own error tolerance, so the exactness is lost at the last step. \magnus\ takes a route that cannot lose unitarity. Because $\Omega$ is anti-Hermitian, $\mathbb{K} \equiv i\Omega$ is Hermitian, so it diagonalizes with a unitary $\mathbb{V}$, and the exponential follows, \ie,
\begin{align}
 \mathbb{K} &= \mathbb{V}\, {\rm diag}(\lambda_1, \ldots, \lambda_n)\, \mathbb{V}^\dagger \;, \nonumber \\
 \label{equ:expm}
 \exp(\Omega) &= \mathbb{V}\, {\rm diag}\!\left(e^{-i\lambda_1}, \ldots, e^{-i\lambda_n}\right) \mathbb{V}^\dagger \;,
\end{align}
where $\lambda_1, \ldots, \lambda_n$ are the eigenvalues of $\mathbb{K}$, all real-valued. Real eigenvalues make the middle factor a diagonal of pure phases, $\lvert e^{-i\lambda} \rvert = 1$, hence unitary. Conjugating it by the unitary $\mathbb{V}$ preserves that, so $\exp(\Omega)$ is unitary by its form rather than to the accuracy of an approximation. Section \ref{sec:exponential} describes how the \magnus\ kernel evaluates \equ{expm} without an eigensolver at two and three flavors, where the eigenvalues have a usable closed form; four and five take the same equation through a general Hermitian eigendecomposition.

Neither route is unitary to the last bit, because $\mathbb{V}$ itself is computed in floating-point arithmetic. Two numbers say how far from it we are. They do not measure the same thing. One exponential deviates by $\lVert \mathbb{U}^\dagger \mathbb{U} - \mathbb{1}\rVert = 4 \cdot 10^{-16}$ in the median; over a stack of $4096$ the worst of them reaches $4 \cdot 10^{-15}$ while the median stays where it was: the workload grows the tail and leaves the typical case alone. A probability composes many such factors, so its defect grows with how many: across four decades in the number of points, at two through five flavors, the worst case moves from $3 \cdot 10^{-12}$ to $1.6 \cdot 10^{-11}$. For all practical purposes, \magnus\ preserves unitarity.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{Two quadrature rules for the Magnus expansion}
\label{sec:quadrature}

Evaluating the Magnus recursion, \equ{recursion}, means sampling $\mathbb{A}$ inside each slab; \magnus\ offers two families of quadrature rule to approximate the integrals in the recursion.

\smallskip

\textit{Collocation integrators (default).---}By default \magnus\ does not evaluate \equ{recursion} as it stands. Each $\Omega_k$ there is a $k$-fold integral over $(k-1)$-fold nested commutators, so the integration and the commutator count both grow steeply with $k$; the sixth-order truncation alone would call for four such integrals. The collocation rules presented below replace them. They define $\Omega_{\rm GL}^{(p)}$, each of which is a quadrature approximation to a \emph{truncation} of the Magnus series in \equ{magnus}, assembled from a few samples of $\mathbb{A}$ at Gauss-Legendre nodes across a slab width, $h$, together with a fixed set of commutators among those samples. Its label is the order $p$ that it attains, not the number of terms that it keeps, \ie,
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:gl_definition}
 \Omega_{\rm GL}^{(p)}
 =
 \sum_{k=1}^{m_p} \Omega_k + \mathcal{O}\!\left(h^{p+1}\right) \;,
\end{equation}
where $m_p = 1,\, 2,\, 4,\, 6$ for $p = 2,\, 4,\, 6,\, 8$, with $p$ always even. The error in \equ{gl_definition} comes from the next pair of Magnus terms of \equ{magnus}, which is what fixes the order at $p$.  \ref{app:terms} derives the sets of Magnus commutators retained in the collocation integrators and the required evenness of $p$.  (While the construction of collocation integrators is not unique to Gauss-Legendre quadrature, collocation terms written under different quadrature schemes cannot be interchanged or mixed.)

What the collocation provides is economy of computation: for a given truncation of the Magnus series in \equ{magnus}, fewer commutators and instances of the Hamiltonian need to be evaluated via collocation compared to the straightforward application of the series, as we show below.  
% Because the quadrature is matched to the truncation, 
% Collocation integration needs far fewer Hamiltonian evaluations than a general rule for the same accuracy. 
It is simultaneously the fastest and the most accurate choice whenever $\mathbb{H}$ is smooth inside a slab. This is the case, \eg, inside the Earth, where the density is smooth between layer boundaries and \magnus\ puts the slab edges on the boundaries themselves (Sec.~\ref{sec:refinement}).

% \Eg, only three evaluations of $\mathbb{A}$ are needed to reproduce a four-term truncation; and only four to reproduce a six-term one.  
% from the sizes of the $\Omega_k$.  

% About the slab midpoint, each $\Omega_k$ carries only odd powers of $h$, since reversing the traversal inverts the evolution operator $\mathbb{U}$ and so changes the sign of $\Omega$ term by term. A one-step method with that symmetry converges at an even order\ \cite{Hairer:2006gni}, so no odd-order scheme of this family exists. Therefore, the terms $\Omega_{\rm GL}^{(p)}$ in \equ{gl_definition} exist only for even values of $p$.

We use $\Omega_{\rm GL}^{(p)}$ as \equ{magnus} uses $\Omega$. On the $j$-th slab, spanning $l_{j-1}$ to $l_j$ with width $h = l_j - l_{j-1}$, the evolution operator is
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:slab_exponential}
 \mathbb{U}_j = \exp\left[\Omega_{\rm GL}^{(p)}(l_j, l_{j-1})\right] \;,
\end{equation}
one matrix exponential per slab, with the $\mathbb{U}_j$ time-ordered. Like for the Magnus series itself (see Sec.~\ref{sec:unitarity}), truncating the series $\Omega_{\rm GL}^{(p)}$ inside the exponential leaves $\mathbb{U}_j$ exactly unitary.

Following \Ref\ \cite{Blanes:2000bit}, Magnus orders 2, 4, 6, and 8 are reached from only one, two, three, or four evaluations of $\mathbb{A}$ per slab, taken at the Gauss-Legendre nodes.
% , with the quadrature order matched exactly to the truncation order.
\magnus\ implements all four. 
(These are \textit{not} the commutator-free family of \Refs\ \cite{Blanes:2006cf, Alvermann:2011cf}, which replaces commutators by products of exponentials of linear combinations of the $\mathbb{A}_i$.) With $\mathbb{A}_j \equiv -i \mathbb{H}_j$ the Hamiltonian at node $j$, the collocation integrators are
\begin{align}
 \label{equ:gl}
 \Omega_{\rm GL}^{(2)} &= h\, \mathbb{A}_1 \;, \nonumber \\
 \Omega_{\rm GL}^{(4)} &= \frac{h}{2}\left(\mathbb{A}_1 + \mathbb{A}_2\right)
 + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{12}\, h^2 \left[\mathbb{A}_2, \mathbb{A}_1\right] \;, \nonumber \\
 \Omega_{\rm GL}^{(6)} &= \alpha_1 + \frac{\alpha_3}{12}
 + \frac{1}{240} \left[-20\,\alpha_1 - \alpha_3 + \mathbb{C}_1,\,
 \alpha_2 + \mathbb{C}_2\right] \;, \nonumber \\
 \Omega_{\rm GL}^{(8)} &= \alpha_1 + \frac{\alpha_3}{12}
 - \frac{7}{120}\,\mathbb{C}_3 + \frac{1}{360}\,\mathbb{C}_6 \;.
\end{align}
Orders 2 and 4 follow \Ref\ \cite{Blanes:2000bit}; orders 6 and 8 are the commutator-optimized forms of \Ref\ \cite{Blanes:2002opt}, the order-6 one also reproduced in the review of \Ref\ \cite{Blanes:2008xlr}. Orders 4, 6, and 8 need 1, 3, and 6 commutators, the fewest possible at each order\ \cite{Blanes:2002opt}. \tabl{collocation} collects the $\alpha_i$ and the $\mathbb{C}_i$ that build the last two of \equ{gl}; the two columns are different objects on different nodes, and cannot be mixed.

(We have found no collocation scheme of this form at order 10 or above. Reference\ \cite{Blanes:2000bit} only tabulates the size of the order-10 problem---80 determining equations after time-symmetry, against 27 at order 8; \Refs\ \cite{Blanes:2002opt, Blanes:2008xlr} stop at order 8. The commutator-free family does not reach higher either, \Refs\ \cite{Blanes:2006cf, Alvermann:2011cf} optimizing up to order 8 as well. Order 8 appears to be where the optimized literature ends. However, given how little order 8 gains over order 6 in \figu{smooth_reach} later, order 10 would rarely be worth reaching for in neutrino oscillation probabilities.)

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

At order 8 the corresponding weights are $w_1 = \frac{1}{2} - \frac{1}{6}\sqrt{5/6}$ and $w_2 = \frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{6}\sqrt{5/6}$, and we write $d_i \equiv w_i v_i$, $\mathbb{S}_1 = \mathbb{A}_1 + \mathbb{A}_4$, $\mathbb{S}_2 = \mathbb{A}_2 + \mathbb{A}_3$, $\mathbb{R}_1 = \mathbb{A}_4 - \mathbb{A}_1$, $\mathbb{R}_2 = \mathbb{A}_3 - \mathbb{A}_2$}
\end{table*}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\smallskip

\textit{Cumulative quadrature.---}The alternative rule to collocation is cumulative quadrature. Here, \magnus\ samples $\mathbb{A}$ on a uniform grid of $m$ points inside each slab ($m = 50$ by default) and integrates with the trapezoid or Simpson rule, selectable. The points are $s_i = l_{j-1} + i\,\Delta s$, spaced by $\Delta s = h/(m-1)$. The $k$-th order term of the series is
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:cum_definition}
 \Omega_{\rm cum}^{(k)}
 =
 \sum_{j=1}^{k} \widehat{\Omega}_j \;.
\end{equation}
Each $\widehat{\Omega}_j$ corresponds to the Magnus term $\Omega_k$ in \equ{recursion}, after replacing the integral by a quadrature sum over the grid, \ie,
\begin{align}
 \label{equ:cum_weights}
 \widehat{\Omega}_1 &= \Delta s \sum_{i=0}^{m-1} w_i\, \mathbb{A}(s_i) \;, \nonumber \\
 \widehat{\Omega}_k &= \sum_{j=1}^{k-1} \frac{B_j}{j!}\, \Delta s \sum_{i=0}^{m-1} w_i\, S_k^{(j)}(s_i) \;,
\end{align}
where the $B_j$ are the Bernoulli numbers of \equ{recursion}, in the same $B_1 = -1/2$ convention, and the $S_k^{(j)}$ are the nested-commutator combinations defined immediately below it. The $w_i$ are the composite trapezoid or Simpson weights (for Simpson, an even $m$ carries a corrected final interval rather than the textbook one). The label $k$ in \equ{cum_definition} counts the terms kept outright, whereas that of the collocation in \equ{gl_definition} counts the error order attained.

The slab evolution operator then follows as in \equ{slab_exponential},
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:slab_exponential_cum}
 \mathbb{U}_j = \exp\left[\Omega_{\rm cum}^{(k)}(l_j, l_{j-1})\right] \;.
\end{equation}
Thus, the two families of quadrature differ only in how that exponent is built. For the same requested accuracy on a smoothly varying Hamiltonian, the cumulative quadrature is slower than collocation, but it is fully general. Therefore, it is the safer choice if $\mathbb{A}$ has a narrow feature---a kink or a jump \emph{inside} a slab---that the Gauss-Legendre nodes might miss. Also, cumulative quadrature is the only route above order 8 in \magnus. It runs to order 10, the highest \magnus\ implements (Sec.~\ref{sec:expansion}); a request beyond that is refused rather than rounded down.
% Requesting more from that path raises an error rather than quietly returning an order-8 result.

\smallskip

\textit{Choosing a quadrature.---}In \magnus, the two dials that select which quadrature rule to use are {\tt magnus\_exp\_order} and {\tt integration\_method}, the latter taking {\tt 'gl'} for collocation and {\tt 'simpson'} or {\tt 'trapezoid'} for the cumulative. \tabl{orders} lists what each delivers. 

The two paths interpret {\tt magnus\_exp\_order} differently. On Gauss-Legendre, it names the order the method delivers, so order 6 is a sixth-order method, \ie, its error over the whole trajectory falls as $h^{6}$ in the slab width or, equivalently, as $N_{\rm slabs}^{-6}$ in the number of slabs, since a trajectory of length $L$ cut into equal slabs has $N_{\rm slabs} = L/h$. We derive in $h$ and measure in $N_{\rm slabs}$; the two are equivalent. On Simpson (or trapezoid), {\tt magnus\_exp\_order} names instead the last term $\Omega_k$ keeps. The method delivers an error order that runs ahead of that index (\ref{app:terms} explains why): \eg, order 6 on Simpson keeps $\Omega_5$ and $\Omega_6$ and is an eighth-order method, with the error falling as $h^8$. Those two extra orders are not a virtue of Simpson's rule. They come from $\Omega_5$ and $\Omega_6$, which the Simpson path keeps and the collocation path does not; the collocation path reaches its sixth order from three samples of $\mathbb{A}$ per slab, where Simpson needs a grid of them, making Simpson the more expensive choice. Above order 8 no collocation scheme exists, so order 10 runs on Simpson or trapezoid exclusively.

No value of $p$ in \tabl{orders} is odd, on either path. The reason is the Magnus terms scale in pairs. About the slab midpoint, each $\Omega_k$ carries only odd powers of $h$, which pushes every odd-indexed term up to the power of its even successor---$\Omega_3$ and $\Omega_4$ both at $h^{5}$, $\Omega_5$ and $\Omega_6$ at $h^{7}$, and so on (\ref{app:terms}). Therefore, keeping $\Omega_7$ without $\Omega_8$ leaves the error unchanged. A request for an odd-valued {\tt magnus\_exp\_order} on the cumulative path delivers the order of the even request just below it, at the cost of one more term; on the collocation path, no odd scheme exists at all, so an odd-valued request runs the next even one.

\magnus\ defaults to {\tt 'gl'} at order 4. Choosing a higher order does not come not free: the adaptive refinement internal of \magnus\ turns it into fewer slabs, but each slab costs more, so raising the order pays only once the tolerance is tight enough for the saving in slabs to outweigh the extra work in each. Across Earth, solar, and exponential density profiles, order 6 runs $0.96$ to $1.12$ times as fast as order 4 at a requested tolerance ($\texttt{rtol}$) of $10^{-4}$. At a tolerance of $10^{-8}$, it runs $1.08$ to $1.93$ times as fast.  Section~\ref{sec:timing_protocol} details our time-measurement protocol. Going down to order 2 is almost never worthwhile: at a tolerance of $10^{-8}$ on an Earth chord, it needs thousands of slabs where order 6 needs about a hundred.
