%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{Slab composition}
\label{sec:order_is_the_slab_product}

We cut a trajectory into $N_{\rm slabs}$ slabs, evaluate the expansion in each, and compose the slab operators in position order,
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:composition}
 \mathbb{U}_{\rm tot}
 =
 \mathbb{U}_{N_{\rm slabs}} \cdots \mathbb{U}_2\, \mathbb{U}_1 \;,
\end{equation}
with the slab crossed \emph{first} standing \emph{rightmost}, since each operator acts on the state to its right. Each factor is unitary, so the product is too, whatever their number and whatever the accuracy of each. The order of the factors matters whenever the slab Hamiltonians do not commute, which on a varying profile is always. How much it matters depends on the Hamiltonian: reversing the profile transposes $\mathbb{U}_{\rm tot}$, which leaves every survival probability alone and swaps $P_{\nu_\alpha \to \nu_\beta}$ with $P_{\nu_\beta \to \nu_\alpha}$; with a nonzero CP-violation phase, the survival probabilities move too. 

\smallskip

\textit{Traditional slab approximation.---}Take $\mathbb{H}$ to be constant across each slab. Every commutator in \equ{recursion} then vanishes, the series stops at $\Omega_1 = -i\,\mathbb{H}\,h$, and \equ{gl} at order 2 reads $\Omega_{\rm GL}^{(2)} = h\,\mathbb{A}_1$ with its single node at the slab midpoint, so that
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:slab_approx}
 \mathbb{U}_j = \exp\left[-i\,\mathbb{H}(l_j^{\rm mid})\,h\right] \;.
\end{equation}
Composed as in \equ{composition}, this is the traditional slab approximation: the varying profile replaced by constant-Hamiltonian slabs, each read at its midpoint. Therefore, the traditional slab approximation is the order-2 Magnus method, slab for slab.

This product was analyzed for a periodically varying density in the seminal work of \Refs\ \cite{Akhmedov:1988kd, Krastev:1989ix}, where it was identified as being responsible for produceing the parametric resonance, and it was solved in closed form for the two-layer castle-wall profile in \Ref\ \cite{Akhmedov:1998ui}. For standard oscillations, each slab has the exact solution for uniform matter\ \cite{Ohlsson:1999xb}; for arbitrary standard and non-standard Hamiltonians, {\tt NuOscProbExact}\ \cite{Bustamante:2019ggq, NuOscProbExact} evaluates it in closed form for two, three, and four flavors. It is also what several publicly available oscillation codes do over the Earth: {\tt Prob3++}\ \cite{Prob3pp}, {\tt GLoBES}\ \cite{GLoBES}, {\tt OscProb}\ \cite{OscProb}, {\tt CUDAProb3}\ \cite{Kallenborn:2019ilo}.

Where the medium really is piecewise constant, \equ{slab_approx} is exact: each factor is exact, so the product is, however many slabs there are\ \cite{Bustamante:2019ggq}. The approximation enters only where the density varies \emph{within} a slab. What is dropped there is the commutator of $\mathbb{H}$ with itself at two positions inside the same slab. The commutator between neighboring slabs is never dropped, since a product of exponentials crosses one slab and then the next exactly, however far $\mathbb{H}$ jumps between them.  

\smallskip

\textit{Beyond Magnus order 2.---}Magnus orders 4, 6, and 8 put back the in-slab commutators that the traditional, order-2 slab approximation drops. Their errors fall as $h^{4}$, $h^{6}$, and $h^{8}$ in the slab width $h$, against $h^{2}$ at order 2, so halving $h$ divides them by $2^{4}$, $2^{6}$, and $2^{8}$ where the slab approximation gains only $2^{2}$. 

The lower-right panel of \figu{phase_vs_profile} shows what that buys on one exponentially falling density profile: at a thousand slabs, order 2 reaches a tolerance in the returned probabilities of around $10^{-6}$; order 4 reaches some four decades below it; and order 6 reaches the round-off floor, which order 2 never reaches on the panel and order 6 reaches with about a hundred slabs.

% Equation (\ref{equ:composition}) is also where this method and a closed-form slab code meet, more closely than either literature suggests. At order 2, \equ{gl} gives $\Omega_{\rm GL}^{(2)} = h\,\mathbb{A}_1$ with its single node at the slab midpoint, so $\mathbb{U}_j = \exp[-i\,\mathbb{H}(l_j^{\rm mid})\,h]$. That is a piecewise-constant composition with the density read at each midpoint, which is exactly what a closed-form slab code computes. We have checked the identity numerically: over twenty-seven slab counts spanning four decades, the order-2 Magnus method and an independently written midpoint slab product agree to between $10^{-15}$ and $10^{-13}$, which is round-off, as it should be for two evaluations of one formula. The lower right panel of \figu{phase_vs_profile} shows the consequence: the order-2 curve falls as $N_{\rm slabs}^{-2}$, the rate of the staircase.

% A closed-form slab code on a continuously varying profile is therefore running Magnus at order 2 without calling it that. Its second-order convergence---halving the slab width buys a factor of four---is a property of that order, not of the closed form that evaluates each step. What \magnus\ adds on the same slab chain is orders 4, 6, and 8, whose errors fall by sixteen, sixty-four, and more per halving. At $1\,024$ slabs in the panel, order 2 stands at $4 \cdot 10^{-6}$, order 4 at $10^{-10}$, and order 6 at $4 \cdot 10^{-15}$; order 2 never reaches $10^{-8}$ on the panel, while order 6 is below it by $128$ slabs. Separately, \magnus\ adds the flavor counts and Hamiltonians no closed form can express, which is most of Section \ref{sec:examples}.

% Two errors compete along each curve in that panel. The truncation error falls as $N_{\rm slabs}^{-p}$ at order $p$; the round-off of the composition grows with $N_{\rm slabs}$. Their sum passes through a minimum and then settles on a floor, which the panel shows as a plateau. The minimum comes sooner the higher the order: at $8\,192$ slabs for order 4, $1\,448$ for order 6 on Gauss--Legendre, $724$ for order 6 on Simpson, and $362$ for orders 8 and 10, all settling near $7 \cdot 10^{-14}$. Order 2 alone never turns: its truncation error is still the larger of the two at $16\,384$ slabs, where it has reached only $1.7 \cdot 10^{-8}$.

% The floor belongs to no order. Composing $N_{\rm slabs}$ double-precision unitary factors, each in error by a relative $\varepsilon$, accumulates those errors incoherently. We isolate the composition by multiplying the same slab operators once in double precision and once at fifty digits, so that the truncation cancels exactly: the difference grows from $1.5 \cdot 10^{-16}$ at two slabs to $1.6 \cdot 10^{-14}$ at $16\,384$, close to $\sqrt{N_{\rm slabs}}$. The curves floor a few times higher than that because each factor is itself formed in double precision before it is multiplied in. The panel shows the same thing directly: beyond about a thousand slabs, orders 6, 8, and 10 on Simpson return \emph{identical} numbers at every plotted point. Refining past the minimum costs accuracy instead of buying it, so the useful range of $N_{\rm slabs}$ ends where the plateau begins. A tolerance tighter than the floor is met there only because successive refinement levels agree at round-off, not because the answer has improved, which is one more reason to read a tolerance as a stopping criterion (Sec.~\ref{sec:refinement}).

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{Tolerance and adaptive refinement}
\label{sec:refinement}

\textit{What tolerance means.---}By default, for a requested tolerance, \magnus\ chooses the slab count itself that is needed to satisfy said tolerance. It computes the probability matrix, increases the number of slabs, recomputes, and stops when two successive levels agree in every entry. Writing $P^{(n)}_{\alpha\beta}$ for the $\nu_\alpha \to \nu_\beta$ probability computed at the $n$-th refinement step, the criterion is
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:refinement_stop}
 \left\lvert P^{(n)}_{\alpha\beta} - P^{(n-1)}_{\alpha\beta} \right\rvert
 \leq
 {\tt atol} + {\tt rtol}\, \left\lvert P^{(n-1)}_{\alpha\beta} \right\rvert
 \quad \text{for all } \alpha, \beta \;,
\end{equation}
with the absolute ({\tt atol}) and relative ({\tt rtol}) tolerance ${\tt atol} = {\tt rtol} = 10^{-3}$ by default. We call that sequence of grids the \emph{refinement ladder}.

\smallskip

\textit{The refinement ladder.---}In the ladder, every level below the one that converges is computed and discarded. For instance, on an Earth chord at three flavors and standard oscillations, reaching a requested accuracy via the ladder takes about four times longer than reaching it by directly providing the right slab count, set in advance.
% , at every accuracy from $10^{-5}$ to $10^{-10}$. 
(In \magnus, that count is available after any converged call to the function that computes the probability, {\tt osc\_prob}, which we introduce later. Passing that value as {\tt n\_slabs} together with ${\tt rtol} = {\tt atol} = {\tt None}$ then makes \magnus\ evaluate once, at that count, and skip the ladder.)

Four devices keep the ladder short.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textit{A physics-informed start.} Before refining, \magnus\ estimates the total phase accumulated along the trajectory, from $\lVert \Omega_1 \rVert_2$, and divides it into slabs of width equal to about one oscillation cycle each. That is the coarsest slab count at which the expansion is guaranteed to converge (Sec.~\ref{sec:applicability}), so the ladder begins there and refines as the Hamiltonian profile requires.
\item \textit{Warm starts.} In a scan of probabilities over neutrino energy or arrival direction, each point starts from the count at which the previous point converged.
\item \textit{Slab edges on the discontinuities.} A slab that contains a jump of the Hamiltonian---brought about, \eg, by a density jump---converges slowly, whatever order was requested: the quadrature inside the slab integrates across the jump, and no order of the expansion repairs that. With an edge placed on every jump, each slab holds a smooth Hamiltonian again, and the error of every order falls as $h^{p}$ as the order promises. Where the edges are placed therefore matters more than the order itself. The Earth is where this matters most, since every neutrino trajectory through it crosses the layer boundaries of the density profile given by the Preliminary Reference Earth Model (PREM); Section~\ref{sec:declaring_edges} describes how \magnus\ places the edges on those boundaries itself, how a caller declares a jump, and how much the placement improves the accuracy.
\item \textit{A caller-supplied minimum.} An {\tt n\_slabs} passed together with a tolerance is a lower bound on the ladder, not a replacement for it: the refinement starts from that count instead of from the seed. This is also the remedy for a profile with features narrower than any slab, turbulent or noisy, which no level of the ladder can see and which therefore no agreement between levels can detect.
\end{enumerate}

\smallskip

\textit{The refinement cap.---}The ladder does not run without limit. It stops at a maximum slab count, {\tt max\_n\_slabs}, set by default to $20\,000$ on the collocation path and $2\,000$ on the cumulative one, whose slabs cost more. If that limit is reached before the tolerance is met, \magnus\ returns the answer from the last level and warns, saying how far from converged it stopped as a multiple of the tolerance. The warning cannot be switched off, because the returned answer is unitary to round-off like every other and can look entirely plausible while being wrong. Section \ref{sec:diagnostics} reports how often those warnings fire, and how often an answer comes back wrong without one.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{The matrix exponential}
\label{sec:exponential}

Every slab ends in a matrix exponential, $\mathbb{U}_j = \exp[\Omega_{\rm GL}^{(p)}]$ or $\mathbb{U}_j = \exp[\Omega_{\rm cum}^{(k)}]$, from \equ{slab_exponential} or \equ{slab_exponential_cum}, and the exponent is anti-Hermitian in either case. Evaluating the exponential is a large part of the cost of a slab. The standard route is to diagonalize the exponent, with a library routine such as {\tt numpy.linalg.eigh}, which returns eigenvalues and eigenvectors, and exponentiate the eigenvalues. That routine costs about a $\mu$s per $3 \times 3$ matrix however many matrices are handed to it at once, because internally it loops over them. What can be done to reduce the cost depends on the size of the matrix.

\smallskip

\textit{A closed-form kernel at two and three flavors.---}The exponential of a $d \times d$ matrix is a polynomial in that matrix of degree $d - 1$ (the Cayley--Hamilton theorem), with coefficients that depend only on the eigenvalues, and at these sizes the eigenvalues have closed forms\ \cite{Zaglauer:1988gz}. No eigenvectors are needed. We carry that polynomial as a compiled kernel (using {\tt numba}), similarly to {\tt NuOscProbExact}~\cite{NuOscProbExact}. Measured against {\tt eigh} under the protocol of Sec.~\ref{sec:timing_protocol}, it is about seven times faster at three flavors and thirteen at two flavors on the exponential alone, and about twice as fast end-to-end on an energy scan through the Earth. 
% The gap between those two factors is the part of each slab's work that is not the exponential, about two fifths of it. The factor of two is what a caller gains, and any further gain has to come from the rest.

% \smallskip

% \textit{What the caller gains.---}The two factors differ because the exponential is only part of the work. Speeding up a fraction $f$ of a calculation by a factor $s$ makes the whole faster by $[(1-f) + f/s]^{-1}$, a dilution known as Amdahl's law; with $s \approx 7$ and an end-to-end gain of about two, the exponential comes out at roughly three fifths of the work per slab. The seven describes the exponential, the two describes the package, and it is the two that a caller gains.

\smallskip

\textit{Accuracy near degeneracy.---}The closed form also gains accuracy where traditional eigenvector methods lose it. The coefficients contain ratios of the form $(e^{-i\lambda_j} - e^{-i\lambda_k})/(\lambda_j - \lambda_k)$, where $\lambda_j$ and $\lambda_k$ are eigenvalues. A naive evaluation computes this as a small difference divided by a small gap. With $\lambda_j - \lambda_k = 2x$ the same ratio is a phase times $\sin x / x$, which is regular at $x = 0$. This is what \magnus\ evaluates, so a nearly degenerate spectrum needs no special treatment. On random Hermitian Hamiltonians with the two closest eigenvalues pushed together by hand, the error is constant over ten orders of magnitude in the gap.
% , compared against a sixty-digit reference calculation.

The closed form fails in one region. For a $3 \times 3$ matrix the eigenvalues are the roots of the characteristic polynomial, a cubic, and the closed form obtains them through the trigonometric solution of that cubic, which involves the arccosine of a combination of the matrix invariants~\cite{MacFarlane:1968vc}. Where that combination approaches $\pm 1$, which happens when the eigenvalue spectrum is nearly degenerate and the norm of the matrix is large, the arccosine amplifies round-off in its argument and the eigenvalues lose several digits in accuracy (compared to a sixty-digit reference calculation). \magnus\ detects that region and hands such matrices to {\tt eigh}. In every scenario in this paper, the hand-off is never taken.

\smallskip

\textit{Four and five flavors, and beyond.---}For a $4 \times 4$ matrix the characteristic polynomial is a quartic, whose roots have a closed form too unwieldy to be worth evaluating; for a $5 \times 5$ it is a quintic, whose roots have no closed form at all (as demonstrated in the Abel-Ruffini theorem). At these sizes, and at any larger number of flavors, \magnus\ therefore diagonalizes numerically, with an iterative eigensolver that treats all the slabs of a trajectory at once and starts the iteration for each slab from the eigenvectors found for the previous one (Sec.~\ref{sec:smooth_comparison}).

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{Adiabatic transport with exact Magnus patches}
\label{sec:hybrid}

The cost of computing a probability with the Magnus expansion grows slowly with the accumulated phase, $\Phi$ in \equ{phase}, but it does grow (Sec.~\ref{sec:phase_vs_profile}). A solar neutrino of a few MeV accumulates a phase of $10^{3}$ to $10^{6}$ radians on its way out of the Sun, and at the upper end of that range the refinement ladder can reach its slab limit before the tolerance is met. Raising the limit would work, at a cost that grows with the phase. The physics offers a better solution.

Away from a \emph{level crossing}---where two eigenvalues of $\mathbb{H}$ approach each other---the neutrino state follows the instantaneous eigenstates of $\mathbb{H}$ as the density changes. This is adiabatic evolution. For standard oscillations in matter it is the content of the Mikheyev--Smirnov--Wolfenstein description of solar neutrinos\ \cite{Wolfenstein:1977ue, Mikheev:1986gs, Parke:1986jy, Bethe:1986ej, Kuo:1989qe}, but it holds for any Hermitian Hamiltonian, and \magnus\ applies it to any: standard, with new interactions, or with extra flavors. It uses adiabatic evolution where it holds and the pure Magnus expansion described earlier where it does not.

\smallskip

\textit{Adiabatic transport.---}At each position $l$ along the neutrino trajectory, we diagonalize the Hamiltonian, $\mathbb{H}(l) = \mathbb{W}(l)\,{\rm diag}(\lambda_k)\,\mathbb{W}^\dagger(l)$, where the $\lambda_k$ are its eigenvalues and $\mathbb{W}(l)$ is the unitary matrix whose columns are its eigenvectors, \ie, the mixing matrix at that position. Adiabatic evolution from $l_0$ to $l_1$ is then
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:adiabatic}
 \mathbb{U}_{\rm ad}(l_1, l_0)
 =
 \mathbb{W}(l_1)\,{\rm diag}\!\left(e^{-i\Phi_k(l_1)}\right)\mathbb{W}^\dagger(l_0) \;,
\end{equation}
where $\Phi_k = \int_{l_0}^{l_1} \lambda_k\,dl$ is the phase accumulated by the $k$-th eigenstate. In \equ{adiabatic}, the phase enters through $e^{-i\Phi_k}$, so an error of a few tenths of a radian in $\Phi_k$ matters, however large $\Phi_k$ is. When $\Phi_k$ is $10^{6}$ radians, that means the integral must be computed to a few parts in $10^{7}$. We adopt Simpson's rule for the integration. (On a grid of a few thousand points, Simpson's rule reaches that accuracy while the trapezoid rule does not. Instead, because the trapezoid error varies smoothly with energy, it produces a small, smooth shift in the probability that could be mistaken for a physical departure from adiabatic evolution.)

The eigenvectors in $\mathbb{W}$ are each defined only up to a phase. We fix those phases along the grid by a simple rule. Let $v_k(l_i)$ be the $k$-th eigenvector at the $i$-th grid point. We multiply it by the phase that makes the inner product $v_k^\dagger(l_{i-1})\, v_k(l_i)$ real and positive. A phase does not change the modulus of the inner product, only its direction in the complex plane, so this choice gives the inner product the largest real part it can have, equal to its modulus. Since $\lVert v_k(l_i) - v_k(l_{i-1}) \rVert^2 = 2 - 2\,{\rm Re}\,[v_k^\dagger(l_{i-1})\, v_k(l_i)]$ for unit vectors, the same choice makes the distance between the two eigenvectors as small as possible. 

Besides the phase $\Phi_k$ from its eigenvalue, an eigenstate that follows a changing Hamiltonian picks up a second phase, which depends only on how its eigenvector turns along the path: the geometric, or Berry, phase. The rule above transports each eigenvector along the path step by step, so this second phase is built into \equ{adiabatic} without a separate formula. It is present for complex Hamiltonians, where it is a genuine phase, and for real ones, where it reduces to a sign. Finally, \equ{adiabatic} is unitary on any grid, since $\mathbb{W}(l_1)$, $\mathbb{W}^\dagger(l_0)$, and the diagonal matrix of phases between them are each unitary.

\smallskip

\textit{Finding where adiabaticity fails.---}Adiabatic evolution fails where the Hamiltonian turns faster than the eigenstates can follow, which happens near a level crossing. We locate those regions with the Hellmann-Feynman identities\ \cite{Feynman:1939zza},
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:hf}
 \frac{d\lambda_k}{dl} = \langle v_k \left\vert \frac{d\mathbb{H}}{dl} \right\vert v_k \rangle ,
 \;\;
 \gamma_{jk} = \frac{\lvert\langle v_j \lvert \tfrac{d\mathbb{H}}{dl} \rvert v_k \rangle\rvert}
 {(\lambda_k - \lambda_j)^2} \;.
\end{equation}
The second quantity measures how non-adiabatic the pair of levels $(j, k)$ is: small where the levels are far apart compared with how fast $\mathbb{H}$ changes, large where they approach. (The adiabaticity parameter of the Landau-Zener literature is its inverse.) Both expressions need only the derivative of $\mathbb{H}$, not the derivative of an eigenvector, which would depend on the above arbitrary phase. 

We evaluate $\gamma_{jk}$ for every pair of eigenvalues $(j, k)$ along the whole trajectory. A crossing between any two of them is therefore found, and so are several crossings that happen at the same place, as when three eigenvalues approach each other at once. Around each position where $\gamma_{jk}$ exceeds a threshold, $0.1$ on the first pass, we grow a window outward until $\gamma_{jk}$ falls below the threshold again, so that the window has the physical width of the crossing; overlapping windows are merged. Inside each window, adiabatic evolution is not trusted; the evolution operator across it is computed with the Magnus expansion instead, as described next. Outside the windows, \equ{adiabatic} is used.

\smallskip

\textit{Patching the crossings.---}Across each window we compute the evolution operator with the Magnus expansion, using the refinement ladder of Sec.~\ref{sec:refinement} to reach the requested tolerance. This is the same computation as on any other trajectory, only over a short interval. The full evolution operator is then the product of the pieces in the order they are crossed,
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:hybrid_composition}
 \mathbb{U}
 =
 \mathbb{U}_{\rm ad}\,\mathbb{U}_{\rm patch}\, \cdots \,\mathbb{U}_{\rm ad} \;,
\end{equation}
with $\mathbb{U}_{\rm ad}$ from \equ{adiabatic} between windows and $\mathbb{U}_{\rm patch}$ from the Magnus expansion inside them. Multiplying the pieces introduces no approximation of its own: the evolution across two intervals in succession is the product of the evolutions across each, whatever method computed them. The product is unitary because every factor is. 
% What the crossing gets is therefore a full solution of the evolution equation across it, at the requested tolerance.
% in place of an estimate.

The usual alternative is the Landau--Zener formula\ \cite{Zener:1932ws}, which estimates the probability that a neutrino hops from one eigenstate to the other at a crossing. We do not use it in \magnus. We employ it only as a check: for a crossing where the eigenvalues vary linearly, which is the case the formula describes, the hop probability we compute agrees with it to about $10^{-3}$.
% The product is not approximated by the Landau--Zener formula\ \cite{Zener:1932ws}, the standard estimate of the probability that a neutrino hops from one eigenstate to the other at a crossing. We use that formula only as a check: for a crossing where the eigenvalues vary linearly, the case the formula was derived for, the hop probability we compute agrees with it to a few parts in a thousand.

\smallskip

\textit{Certification.---}The procedure above depends on the threshold on $\gamma_{jk}$ and on the two grids, the one on which $\mathbb{H}$ is diagonalized and the one on which the crossings are searched for. No choice of these is safe for every Hamiltonian, so \magnus\ does not trust a single pass. It computes the probability twice, the second time with the threshold divided by three and both grids twice as fine, and compares the two. If they agree within the requested tolerance, the result is reported as \emph{certified}. If they do not, \magnus\ returns the second result, which is unitary to round-off like every other, and reports that it is not certified.

We validated the adiabatic transport with Magnus patches, certification included, against a tight {\tt DOP853} integration (Runge-Kutta order 8) of the evolution equation on a set of density profiles with zero, one, two separate, and two overlapping crossings, at three, four, and five flavors, with and without CP violation. It runs $30$ to $4800$ times faster than the integration. Its largest error on that set, over certified and uncertified results alike, is $3 \cdot 10^{-3}$ against a requested tolerance of $10^{-4}$. 

Two ways of failing remain, neither specific to \magnus: any method that evaluates the Hamiltonian on a grid of positions has them. First, a crossing narrower than the spacing of the grid falls between its points and is never seen. Second, two passes can agree with each other and both be wrong, so a certified result can still lie outside the tolerance. Section \ref{sec:limitations} measures how often the first happens.
% Two failure modes remain, and both belong to any method that samples the Hamiltonian on a grid. A crossing narrower than the grid spacing is invisible to every diagnostic. And a result can be certified, because two passes agreed, and still be outside the tolerance. Section \ref{sec:limitations} measures the first.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{The phase-averaged limit, in closed form}
\label{sec:averaged}

A neutrino from an astrophysical source arrives with a phase that no experiment resolves. For a TeV neutrino from $100$~Mpc the accumulated phase is of order $10^{16}$ radians, and none of the quantities that fix it---the distance, the size of the source, the energy of the neutrino---is known to anything near the precision it would take to resolve the oscillations. Whatever the phase is, the measurement averages over many cycles of it. Propagating such a neutrino while trying to resolve the oscillation is expensive and unnecessary; what is needed is the average.

\smallskip

\textit{The averaged probability.---}We write the Hamiltonian in its eigenbasis, $\mathbb{H} = \mathbb{V}\,{\rm diag}(\lambda_i)\,\mathbb{V}^\dagger$, with $\mathbb{V}$ the matrix of its eigenvectors. The oscillation probability is then a sum of terms, one for each pair of eigenstates, each oscillating with the phase difference of the pair. Averaging over the phase removes every term with $i \neq j$ and leaves
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:averaged}
 \left\langle P_{\nu_\alpha \to \nu_\beta} \right\rangle
 =
 \sum_i \lvert \mathbb{V}_{\alpha i}\rvert^2\, \lvert \mathbb{V}_{\beta i}\rvert^2 \;.
\end{equation}
Equation (\ref{equ:averaged}) is the exact limit of the probability as $L/E \to \infty$. Computing it requires one diagonalization of $\mathbb{H}$. 

Three properties of \equ{averaged} are worth noting:
\begin{enumerate}
\item It is symmetric under the exchange of $\alpha$ and $\beta$.
\item In vacuum, it is the same for neutrinos and antineutrinos, since the antineutrino probability is obtained by replacing $\mathbb{V}$ with $\mathbb{V}^\ast$, and \equ{averaged} contains only $\lvert \mathbb{V} \rvert^2$. At three flavors, the CP-violation phase, $\delta_{\rm CP}$, still affects the result through the values of $\lvert \mathbb{V}_{\alpha i} \rvert^2$, but it produces no difference between neutrinos and antineutrinos. In matter, neutrinos and antineutrinos have different averaged probabilities, because the matter potential has opposite signs for the two.
\item It does not depend on the baseline. It depends on the energy only through the eigenvectors of $\mathbb{H}$. In vacuum, those are independent of the energy, since the vacuum Hamiltonian is proportional to $1/E$ and a matrix and any multiple of it have the same eigenvectors, so the averaged probability is a constant. In matter, the eigenvectors change with the energy, through the competition between the matter potential and the vacuum term, and so does the averaged probability. A non-standard Hamiltonian may have an entirely different energy dependence.
\end{enumerate}

\smallskip

\textit{Coherent blocks.---}Equation (\ref{equ:averaged}) assumes that the phase between every pair of eigenstates has averaged away. That is a statement about each pair separately. Two eigenvalues close enough that their phase difference stays small over the whole baseline remain coherent, and their cross term survives. Therefore, \magnus\ groups the eigenvalues into blocks that are mutually coherent, sums coherently within each block and incoherently between blocks,
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:averaged_blocks}
 \left\langle P_{\nu_\alpha \to \nu_\beta} \right\rangle
 =
 \sum_b \left\vert \sum_{i \in b} \mathbb{V}^\ast_{\alpha i}\mathbb{V}_{\beta i} \right\vert^2 \;,
\end{equation}
which reduces to \equ{averaged} when every block contains one eigenvalue. The distinction matters whenever two eigenvalues lie close enough that their phase difference does not average out over the baseline, as for a pseudo-Dirac pair or a sterile state with a small mass splitting, and it matters absolutely for a degenerate spectrum: with all eigenvalues equal, nothing oscillates and the probability is the identity, while \equ{averaged} would return a mixture. A pair that is neither coherent nor fully averaged is described by neither equation, and \magnus\ says so instead of returning a number.

\smallskip

\textit{What the average is not.---}It is not the effect of a finite experimental resolution. A spread in baseline or energy that partly suppresses an oscillation without removing it is described by damping factors multiplying each term of the probability\ \cite{Kiers:1995zj, Blennow:2005yk, Ohlsson:2000mj}; that is an operation on the final probability, and \magnus\ does not perform it. Nor is it the same as averaging the probability over an energy band, because $\mathbb{H}$ depends on the energy: averaging over a band averages over different Hamiltonians, while \equ{averaged_blocks} averages the phase of one. In vacuum, the two coincide, since the eigenvectors do not depend on energy. In matter, they differ by an amount set by how fast the eigenvectors change with energy across the band. For standard oscillations that change is largest near a resonance and vanishes again at high density, where the eigenvectors are pinned to the flavor states; for a non-standard Hamiltonian it follows whatever energy dependence that Hamiltonian has.

\smallskip

\textit{A varying Hamiltonian.---}Equations (\ref{equ:averaged}) and (\ref{equ:averaged_blocks}) assume that the Hamiltonian is the same along the whole path, so that one eigenbasis serves both production and detection. When the Hamiltonian varies along the path, as in the Sun, the Earth, and a supernova shock, the two eigenbases differ, and the neutrino may also move from one eigenstate to another at a crossing on the way. The averaged probability is then
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:averaged_varying}
 \left\langle P_{\nu_\alpha \to \nu_\beta} \right\rangle
 =
 \sum_{ij} \lvert \mathbb{V}_{\alpha i}(l_0)\rvert^2\,
 P^{\rm cross}_{ij}\,
 \lvert \mathbb{V}_{\beta j}(l_1)\rvert^2 \;,
\end{equation}
where $\mathbb{V}(l_0)$ and $\mathbb{V}(l_1)$ are the eigenvector matrices at production and at detection, and $P^{\rm cross}_{ij}$ is the probability that a neutrino produced in eigenstate $i$ arrives in eigenstate $j$. Where the evolution is adiabatic, $P^{\rm cross}$ is the identity; across a crossing, it is computed with the Magnus patch of Sec.~\ref{sec:hybrid}. The crossings are located on a grid of probes along the path. A feature narrower than their spacing, such as a sharp density front, would fall between two of them; where the profile has one that could move probability between levels, \magnus\ locates the crossings with the refinement ladder of Sec.~\ref{sec:hybrid} instead, and where no refinement resolves it, it warns that the feature must be declared. This is the standard result for solar neutrinos\ \cite{Kuo:1989qe, Dighe:1999bi}, here for any number of eigenstates and any number of crossings. For a constant Hamiltonian, $\mathbb{V}(l_0) = \mathbb{V}(l_1)$ and $P^{\rm cross}$ is the identity, and \equ{averaged_varying} reduces to \equ{averaged}.

(A varying medium is one reason for the eigenbases at the two ends to differ; \equ{averaged_varying} does not care what the reason is. If the mixing matrix is not unitary, because heavy states have been integrated out, its effective form can differ between the production and the detection process\ \cite{Antusch:2014woa, Blennow:2016jkn, Fong:2022oim}. And if the mixing parameters run with energy, and new physics brings that running into the range an experiment spans, then production and detection at different energies see two evaluations of one matrix\ \cite{Antusch:2003kp, Antusch:2005gp, Bustamante:2010bf, Babu:2021cxe, Babu:2022non, Ge:2024ibn, Bustamante:2026zst}. Equation (\ref{equ:averaged_varying}) accommodates either case with $P^{\rm cross}$ the identity and the two matrices supplied directly. Presently, \magnus\ does not expose that: it computes both eigenbases from the one Hamiltonian it is given.)

Section \ref{sec:sun} shows what this buys on the Sun, and Section \ref{sec:averaging_is_not_estimating} why averaging a scan over energy does not converge to the same number.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Code description}
\label{sec:code}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{Scope and interface}
\label{sec:scope_interface}

\magnus\ is written in Python and requires version 3.10 or later. It depends on {\tt NumPy}\ \cite{Harris:2020xlr}, {\tt SciPy}\ \cite{Virtanen:2019joe}, {\tt joblib}, {\tt Numba}\ \cite{Lam:2015numba}, and {\tt Matplotlib}\ \cite{Hunter:2007ouj}, all installed with it. 
% {\tt Matplotlib} is imported only inside the plotting functions, so {\tt import magnus} does not load it.

\smallskip

\textit{One function, and wrappers around it.---}The central function is {\tt osc\_prob}. It takes a Hamiltonian---either a function ${\tt H\_func}(l)$ of the position that returns a Hermitian matrix of any size, or a constant matrix---and returns the matrix of probabilities $P_{\nu_\alpha \to \nu_\beta}$ for every pair of initial and final flavors. Everything else in \magnus\ is built around {\tt osc\_prob}.

Every other function that \magnus\ exposes to the user builds a Hamiltonian for a particular case and passes it to {\tt osc\_prob}. For convenience to the user \magnus\ provides fifty-six wrappers covering common cases: one for each combination of flavor count (2, 3, 4, or 5), environment (vacuum, constant density, exponential density, the Earth, the Sun), and scenario (standard oscillations, non-standard interactions, Lorentz-invariance violation). They are named by a fixed rule, {\tt osc\_prob\_$\langle n\rangle$nu\_$\langle$environment$\rangle$\_$\langle$scenario$\rangle$}, with the scenario omitted for standard oscillations: \eg, {\tt osc\_prob\_3nu\_sun} and {\tt osc\_prob\_4nu\_earth\_liv}. The physical parameters of each wrapper are passed as named arguments: the mixing angle $\theta_{12}$ as {\tt s12}, the matter density as {\tt rho}, the non-standard coupling $\varepsilon_{e\mu}$ as {\tt eps\_em}. Listing~\ref{lst:constant} shows a wrapper and {\tt osc\_prob} computing the same probability.

\smallskip

\textit{Scans.---}The function {\tt osc\_prob} and every wrapper accept an array of energies, an array of baselines, or both, and return the probabilities for every element. Such a call is cheaper than the same set of probabilities computed one at a time, because \magnus\ exploits three kinds of structure in the scan. First, when the Hamiltonian is the sum of a position-independent term and a matter potential multiplied by a fixed matrix, as in \equ{h_matter}, the potential is sampled once along the path and reused for every energy. Second, when the matter potential is constant, the entire scan reduces to the exponential of a single stack of matrices. Third, when several baselines are requested at one energy, the path to each shorter baseline is contained in the path to the longest, so a single propagation along the longest path yields the probabilities at all of them. Section \ref{sec:engines}, explained later, describes the six computational routes---or \textit{engines}---that implement these three cases, and the order in which they are tried. Any call can report which engine produced its result.

\smallskip

\textit{Errors that explain themselves.---}When a call is malformed, \magnus\ reports what is missing and what it expected instead of raising a bare exception. For instance, omitting the baseline on an Earth chord returns a message saying that the arrival direction will set the chord length but the baseline itself may not be {\tt None}; a misspelled keyword returns the closest valid name together with the full list of keywords the wrapper accepts.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{The \magnus\ architecture}
\label{sec:architecture}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\begin{figure*}[t!]
 \centering
 \includegraphics[width=0.98\textwidth]{architecture.pdf}
 \caption{\label{fig:architecture}The modules of \magnus\ and the path of a request through them. A Hamiltonian enters from the left, either supplied by the user as a function of position or built by {\tt hamiltonians} for one of the implemented scenarios, with the density profile provided by {\tt matter} or {\tt earth}. The request is handled in {\tt oscprob}, which contains the refinement ladder of Section \ref{sec:refinement} and the choice of engine of Section \ref{sec:engines}. From there the computation proceeds along one of two routes: through {\tt magnus}, which evaluates the expansion slab by slab and contains no physics, or through {\tt avgprob} and {\tt adiabatic}, which evaluate no slabs---the first for the phase-averaged limit, the second for transport along the instantaneous eigenstates, which calls {\tt magnus} back to compute the evolution across a level crossing. The dashed arrow to {\tt expmkernels} indicates that this module affects only the speed: a {\tt NumPy} implementation of the same exponential exists behind it, and the test suite verifies that the two agree to round-off}
\end{figure*}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Figure~\ref{fig:architecture} shows the modules of \magnus\ and the path of a request through them, from a Hamiltonian on the left to a probability on the right. A request arrives at the {\tt oscprob} module and leaves it either to {\tt magnus}, which evaluates the expansion slab by slab, to {\tt avgprob}, which computes the phase-averaged limit of the probability, or to {\tt adiabatic}, which transports the neutrino state along the instantaneous eigenstates, returning to {\tt magnus} only to cross a level crossing within non-adiabatic Magnus patches. The route is selected internally by \magnus\ from the form of the request, not by the user; Section \ref{sec:engines} below gives the order in which the candidates are tried.

Two properties of this arrangement are not visible from outside the package. First, {\tt magnus} imports nothing from the rest of it. The module is numerical linear algebra on an arbitrary matrix-valued function $\mathbb{A}(l)$, with no reference to neutrinos, and it can therefore be tested against the recursion of \equ{recursion} without involving any of the probability code. Second, {\tt oscprob} is the only module that imports every other one. The physics, environments, and numerical core are connected in that one module.

Table~\ref{tab:modules} lists the twelve modules under {\tt src/magnus/}, together with the {\tt hamiltonians} subpackage, and the responsibility of each. The largest module is {\tt oscprob}, which holds the wrappers of Section \ref{sec:scope_interface}. To avoid duplicating the same logic in each of them, the functions of {\tt oscprob} are organized in four layers, each calling the one below it.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\begin{figure*}[t!]
 \centering
 \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{layers.pdf}
 \caption{\label{fig:layers}The four layers of {\tt oscprob}, and the path of a request through them. A request enters at one of the named wrappers, which convert their named arguments into a parameter dictionary and pass it to the scenario function of the second layer; that function builds the Hamiltonian and passes it to {\tt osc\_prob\_energy\_baseline}, which calls {\tt osc\_prob} once for every requested energy and baseline; {\tt osc\_prob} runs the refinement ladder of Section \ref{sec:refinement} and selects one of the seven engines of Section \ref{sec:engines} according to the form of the request. The wrappers are a product set: the fourteen environment-and-scenario names listed in the left box exist at each of the four flavor counts, giving $4 \times 14 = 56$ in all. There is no wrapper for non-standard interactions in vacuum, since those interactions require matter}
\end{figure*}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Figure \ref{fig:layers} shows the four layers:
\begin{enumerate}
\item The top layer consists of the named wrappers, such as {\tt osc\_prob\_3nu\_matter\_constant\_density} in Listing~\ref{lst:constant}. Each converts its named arguments into the parameter dictionary expected by the second layer and calls the function there for its scenario.
\item The second layer consists of four functions, one for each physics scenario and each applicable to any number of flavors: {\tt osc\_prob\_vacuum}, {\tt osc\us prob\us matter\us std\us potential}, {\tt osc\_prob\_matter\_nsi}, and {\tt osc\_prob\_liv}. Each builds the Hamiltonian for its scenario and passes it to the third layer.
\item The third layer is {\tt osc\us prob\us energy\us baseline}, which receives the Hamiltonian together with the arrays of energies and baselines and calls the base layer once for each pair, seeding the refinement of each point from the converged slab count of the previous one.
\item The base layer is {\tt osc\_prob}, which computes one probability matrix. It is the only function that invokes the Magnus core; it implements the refinement ladder, validates the input, and handles logging; and it can be called directly with an arbitrary Hamiltonian, as in Listing~\ref{lst:constant}.
\end{enumerate}
Below the base layer, {\tt osc\_prob} selects one of the seven engines of Sec.~\ref{sec:engines} below, according to the form of the request.

The refinement keywords ({\tt n\_slabs}, {\tt max\us n\us slabs}, {\tt t\_breakpoints}, {\tt magnus\_exp\_order}, {\tt integration\us method}, {\tt rtol}, {\tt atol}, {\tt strict\_convergence}, and {\tt n\_jobs}) are declared once, in {\tt osc\_prob}, and reach every wrapper through {\tt **kwargs}. Therefore, they do not appear in the signature of a wrapper, and {\tt help()} on a wrapper does not list them; its docstring names them and refers to {\tt osc\_prob}, where they are documented.

Extending the package usually requires only a new Hamiltonian. {\tt osc\_prob} accepts any function that returns a Hermitian matrix and does not depend on how that matrix was constructed, so a new term can be added to the array returned by an existing builder and the result passed on unchanged. A new function in the second layer is needed only for physics that fits none of the four existing scenarios.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
{\def\arraystretch{1.2}
\begin{table}[t!]
 {\small
 \begin{tabular}{@{}lp{5.0cm}@{}}
 Module & Responsibility \\
 \hline
 {\tt magnus} & The expansion: collocation integrators, cumulative quadrature, slab composition, the unitary exponential \\
 {\tt expmkernels} & Compiled kernels for the exponential: Cayley--Hamilton at $2\times2$ and $3\times3$, batched Jacobi solver at $4\times4$ and $5\times5$ \\
 {\tt expansionterms} & The recursion of \equ{recursion}, in exact rational arithmetic, at any order \\
 {\tt adiabatic} & Adiabatic transport, detection of level crossings, the combined propagator \\

 {\tt matter} & Density profiles, electron density, $V_{\rm CC}$, the matter-potential projector \\
 {\tt hamiltonians} & Mixing matrices and the vacuum, matter, NSI, and LIV Hamiltonians \\
 {\tt globaldefs} & Constants, unit conversions, NuFIT\ \cite{Esteban:2024eli} parameter sets \\

 \end{tabular}}
 \caption{\label{tab:modules}The modules of \magnus\ v1.1.1. {\tt magnus} and {\tt hamiltonians} can be used and tested independently of the probability functions.}
\end{table}}

\subsection{Example: one request, through the layers}
\label{sec:one_request}

Listing~\ref{lst:layers} shows a request passing through the four layers of \figu{layers}: the call {\tt osc\_prob\_3nu\_matter\_constant\_density} (of Listing~\ref{lst:constant}), followed by the four functions it passes through, each abbreviated to the arguments that carry the request on. 

The wrapper collects the oscillation parameters it received by name into a dictionary and calls the second-layer function for standard oscillations in matter, {\tt osc\_prob\_matter\_std\_potential}, with the flavor count, the density, the energy, and the baseline. That function converts the density to the potential $V_{\rm CC}$, builds the Hamiltonian of \equ{h_matter} from the vacuum term and $V_{\rm CC}\,\mathbb{P}$, and passes it to the third layer. There, {\tt osc\_prob\_energy\_baseline} constructs the Hamiltonian at each requested energy, seeds the refinement of each point from the converged slab count of the previous one, and calls {\tt osc\_prob} for every pair of energy and baseline. Finally, {\tt osc\_prob} runs the refinement ladder of Section \ref{sec:refinement} and selects the engine. The two refinement keywords in the request, {\tt rtol} and {\tt atol}, are declared by none of the first three functions; they travel through {\tt **kwargs} until {\tt osc\_prob}, which declares and uses them.

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python, caption={A request through the four layers of \figu{layers}. The first block is the call of Listing~\ref{lst:constant}, with two refinement keywords added; the four blocks below it are the functions it passes through, all defined in the module {\tt magnus.oscprob} ({\tt src/magnus/oscprob.py}), abbreviated to the arguments that carry the request on. The wrapper packs the physical parameters it received by name into a dictionary; the keywords it does not declare, here {\tt rtol} and {\tt atol}, pass through {\tt **kwargs} unchanged until {\tt osc\_prob} consumes them.}, label={lst:layers}, float=tbp]
# The request: physical parameters by name, and
# two refinement keywords, which the wrapper does
# not declare and which travel down in **kwargs
P = osc_prob_3nu_matter_constant_density(
        E, L, rho=3.0,
        density_matter_is_in_g_per_cm3=True,
        s12=osc['s12'], s23=osc['s23'],
        s13=osc['s13'], dCP=osc['dCP'],
        D21=osc['D21'], D31=osc['D31'],
        rtol=1e-8, atol=1e-10)
# here kwargs == {'rtol': 1e-8, 'atol': 1e-10}

# Top layer: the named wrapper
def osc_prob_3nu_matter_constant_density(
        energy, L, rho, s12, s23, s13, dCP,
        D21, D31, ..., **kwargs):
    return osc_prob_matter_std_potential(
        num_flavors=3, rho_func=rho,
        energy=energy, L=L,
        osc_params={'s12': s12, 's23': s23,
                    's13': s13, 'dCP': dCP,
                    'D21': D21, 'D31': D31},
        ..., **kwargs)

# Second layer: the scenario function builds H
def osc_prob_matter_std_potential(
        num_flavors, rho_func, energy, L,
        osc_params, ..., **kwargs):
    htot = ...   # vacuum term + V_CC(l) * P
    return osc_prob_energy_baseline(
        htot, energy, L, ..., **kwargs)

# Third layer: the scan over (energy, baseline)
def osc_prob_energy_baseline(H_func, energy, L,
                             ..., **kwargs):
    for E, baseline in ...:
        P = osc_prob(H_at_energy(E), L0, baseline,
                     **kwargs)
    ...

# Base layer: one probability matrix per point
def osc_prob(H_func, l0, l1, rtol, atol, ...):
    ...   # refinement ladder; rtol, atol used here
\end{lstlisting}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{The seven engines}
\label{sec:engines}

When a user requests a probability from \magnus, by default they do not need to separately select a method to compute it. Behind the interface of Sec.~\ref{sec:scope_interface}, seven engines can answer such a request. Every entry point tries them in a fixed order, from the most specialized to the most general, and uses the first one whose conditions the request satisfies. An engine whose conditions are not satisfied declines the request, which then passes to the next engine in the order. The general Magnus ladder, which comes last, accepts every request.

\smallskip

\textit{How a request is assigned to an engine.}---No argument names the engine that answers; the content of the request decides. One keyword, {\tt strategy}, gates whether the adiabatic engine may take part, and is described below---it selects no engine of its own. A request for an averaged probability with a position-independent Hamiltonian is assigned to the closed-form average (Sec.~\ref{sec:averaged}). An array of energies at a single baseline is assigned to the energy-batched scan, and a single energy at an array of baselines to the cumulative scan. A profile declared as exponential, at two flavors, is assigned to the interaction picture. Slab edges supplied through {\tt t\_slab\_edges}, or a request that specifies no tolerance, exclude the adiabatic engine. Every other request is assigned to the general Magnus ladder. Passing an array instead of looping over its entries therefore changes which engine computes the answer; Section~\ref{sec:cost} measures the difference in cost. We explain the engines below.

The keyword {\tt strategy} is that exception: it gates a single engine rather than choosing among them. It takes three values. The default, {\tt 'auto'}, lets the adiabatic engine attempt any request that meets its conditions; the engine returns its answer only if it can certify it, otherwise the request passes to the next engine. {\tt 'hybrid'} lets it answer whether or not it can certify, provided its other conditions hold; where they do not---declared breakpoints, or no requested tolerance---the request passes on regardless. {\tt 'magnus'} excludes it, so that the request goes directly to the slab-based engines. The accuracy of the default is therefore the accuracy of the certified answers. Section~\ref{sec:hybrid} reports it as the error of the adiabatic engine over the whole validation grid, including every answer it certified.

\smallskip

\textit{The engines.---}Figure~\ref{fig:strategies} and Table\tabl{engines} show the seven engines in the order in which they are tried internally by \magnus. 
\begin{enumerate}

\item \emph{Phase average.} This engine answers every request made with {\tt average=True}, by one of three routes chosen by $\mathbb{H}$. When $\mathbb{H}$ does not depend on position, it returns \equ{averaged}, the limit of $P_{\nu_\alpha \to \nu_\beta}$ as $L/E \to \infty$, in which every interference term between two eigenstates of $\mathbb{H}$ has averaged to zero; this is the quantity a solar or an astrophysical experiment measures. It diagonalizes $\mathbb{H}$ once, numerically, then evaluates \equ{averaged} from the moduli of the eigenvector components: nothing is propagated, no baseline enters, and no average over a range of baselines or energies is taken. When $\mathbb{H}$ varies smoothly along the path, it returns the same limit through \equ{averaged_varying}, transporting along the instantaneous eigenstates with the Magnus patch of Sec.~\ref{sec:hybrid} across every crossing that is not adiabatic. When the profile declares discontinuities, there is no instantaneous eigenbasis, and it propagates for real across a window in energy and averages over it, a different quantity (Sec.~\ref{sec:average_keyword}). See Sec.~\ref{sec:astro} for an example.

\smallskip

\item \emph{Adiabatic $+$ Magnus.} This engine is the adiabatic transport with Magnus patches of Section~\ref{sec:hybrid}. It applies to any Hamiltonian that varies along the trajectory, whatever the origin of the variation, and it is reached through the scenario functions of the second layer, which build $\mathbb{H}$ or accept one from the caller. It is the engine for a large accumulated phase: its cost is set by the grid on which $\mathbb{H}$ is diagonalized and by the crossings it patches, not by the phase, which the general ladder would have to resolve slab by slab. Under {\tt strategy='auto'} it returns an answer only when the certification of Section~\ref{sec:hybrid} succeeds; otherwise the request passes to the next engine. It also declines when slab edges or breakpoints are supplied, when no tolerance is requested, or when $\mathbb{H}$ is not resolved on the grid on which it searches for crossings.

\smallskip

\item \emph{Interaction picture.} At low energy, the vacuum phase is  large, and a slab method must resolve every cycle of it although that part of the problem is solved exactly. Switching to the interaction picture removes this phase. This switch can be applied to any Hamiltonian that separates into a position-independent part and a position-dependent term of the form $V(l)\,\mathbb{M}$, with $\mathbb{M}$ a fixed matrix: the position-independent part is diagonalized once, its evolution is factored out analytically, and only the envelope $V(l)$ remains to be integrated. In \magnus, the position-dependent term is the matter term, so the Hamiltonian is $\mathbb{H}(E, l) = \mathbb{H}_0(E) + V_{\rm CC}(l)\,\mathbb{P}$, where $\mathbb{P}$ is the matter projector (Sec.~\ref{sec:recap}), with non-standard interactions if present, and $\mathbb{H}_0$ collects the vacuum term and any other position-independent contributions, including non-standard ones. 

The engine is specific to an exponential envelope, $V_{\rm CC}(l_0 + s) = V_{\rm CC}(l_0)\,e^{-s/l_{\rm scale}}$, because the integral of the envelope against the free-evolution phase, which the first-order Magnus term requires, is then elementary; for any other envelope it would have to be computed numerically, slab by slab, which is what the engine exists to avoid. The solar density falls close to exponentially over most of the radius, and solar neutrinos, with their large vacuum phase, are the case the engine is for. Over a slab of length $h$ starting at $l_0$, the first-order term is
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:ip_omega1}
 \big(\tilde{\Omega}_1\big)_{jk}(h)
 = -i\, \big(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\big)_{jk}\, V_{\rm CC}(l_0)\,
 \frac{e^{(i \Delta_{jk} - 1/l_{\rm scale})h} - 1}{i \Delta_{jk} - 1/l_{\rm scale}} ,
\end{equation}
where a tilde marks a matrix in the eigenbasis of $\mathbb{H}_0$, $\Delta_{jk} = \lambda_j - \lambda_k$ is the gap between two of its eigenvalues, and $l_{\rm scale}$ is the scale height; the expression holds for $j = k$ as well. Equation~(\ref{equ:ip_omega1}) integrates the exponential envelope exactly over the slab: $V_{\rm CC}(l)$ is not replaced by a constant or a straight line within the slab, as a quadrature would do. The only approximation is the truncation of the series at first order, which is accurate wherever the matter term is a small perturbation on the vacuum splitting, and it improves as the energy falls, since $\Delta_{jk}$ grows as $1/E$. Three conditions have to hold for this engine to take a request: two flavors, a profile built with {\tt exp\_density\_profile}, and no slab edges or breakpoints. (The limit of two flavors comes from truncating the series at first order: with three or more flavors, the terms dropped by the truncation are no longer small, and an implementation would need the second-order term, which \magnus\ does not include.) Even then it keeps the request only if its iteration converges. At an MSW resonance the matter term is no longer small, the iteration fails, and the request passes to the next engine.

\smallskip

\item \emph{Constant Hamiltonian.} When $\mathbb{H}$ does not vary along the trajectory, the Magnus series terminates at its first term: every $\Omega_k$ beyond $\Omega_1$ is a nested commutator of $\mathbb{H}$ with itself, hence zero. The evolution operator is therefore $\exp(-i\mathbb{H}L)$ exactly, with no discretization at all, and a whole scan is one stacked exponential over an $(n_E, d, d)$ array, with $d$ the number of flavors. The engine applies at any flavor count, to a single point as well as to a scan, and it accepts per-point baselines, which the energy-batched scan below does not. Refinement keywords are accepted and ignored, since they can only ask for a refinement of something already exact.

\smallskip

\item \emph{Energy-batched scan.} A scan over many energies at one baseline, computed point by point, traverses the same profile once per energy although the profile does not depend on it. When $\mathbb{H}$ separates as above (\eg, as the standard, non-standard-interaction, and Lorentz-violating Hamiltonians do), this engine samples the potential once per refinement level and shares it across the batch; the quadrature, the commutators, the exponentials, and the slab products carry the energy as a batch dimension, and energies that have converged drop out of further refinement. 

\smallskip

\item \emph{Cumulative scan.} The complementary case is one energy at many baselines. Each requested baseline is a prefix of the next, $\mathbb{U}(0, L_2) = \mathbb{U}(L_1, L_2)\,\mathbb{U}(0, L_1)$, so this engine traverses the profile once and records the running product at every requested baseline, where computing the baselines independently would traverse it $N$ times. The traversal is chunked and each recorded product is converted to a probability at once, so that peak memory scales with the chunk and not with the number of baselines.

\smallskip

\item \emph{General Magnus ladder.} The last engine is the refinement ladder method of Section~\ref{sec:refinement}. It assumes only a Hermitian $\mathbb{H}(l)$: it lays slabs along the trajectory, builds the Mangus expansion on each, composes them in order, and raises the slab count until the probabilities computed in two successive stages agree to the requested tolerance. It is reached whenever no engine above it applies.
\end{enumerate}

(For two flavors on an exponential profile, the evolution equation also has an exact solution in confluent hypergeometric functions\ \cite{Petcov:1987xd, Toshev:1987jw, Petcov:1987zj}, one of the few density profiles for which one exists\ \cite{Notzold:1987cq, Kim:1987ss, Balantekin:1988aq}; \magnus\ does not use it.)

Agreement among the engines is not a check, since the general ladder, the cumulative scan, and the energy-batched scan share one slab kernel; the reference to compare against has to come from outside \magnus, as we show later.

\smallskip

\textit{Seeing which engine answered.}---When an engine declines a request and the next one takes it, nothing is reported to the user. This happens on ordinary calls, where a warning each time would be noise. Nevertheless, any call made through a scenario function or one of its wrappers can be asked which engine answered it. A dictionary passed as {\tt strategy\_info} returns with three entries: the engine that answered; whether, if it was the adiabatic engine, the answer was certified; and the engines that declined, each with its reason. This makes an answer traceable. A result that moves when a point is added to a scan, or a call that suddenly costs more, is a change of engine, and the dictionary names it. 

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\begin{figure*}[t!]
 \centering
 \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{strategies.pdf}
 \caption{\label{fig:strategies}The seven \magnus\ engines that can answer a probability request, in the order in which they are tried. Each row draws what its engine does to the trajectory: for the phase average, $\langle P \rangle$ read from the eigenbases of $\mathbb{H}$, exact and with no baseline when one eigenbasis serves the whole path, and otherwise from the eigenbases at the two ends and the probability of each crossing between them, with no phase tracked in either case; adiabatic transport with an exact Magnus patch across the resonance; the fast vacuum phase removed analytically before a single Magnus pass; one exponential for the whole path where $\mathbb{H}$ does not vary; one evaluation of the profile shared across multiple energies; one pass returning multiple baselines; and the general Magnus ladder, refining until two successive stages agree. Shading represents the matter density. An engine declines a request whose conditions it does not meet; in that case, the request passes to the next engine, so the last engine (general Magnus ladder) is reached whenever no other applies; \tabl{engines} gives the conditions.}
\end{figure*}

 \hline

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{Batching and parallelization}
\label{sec:batching}

In \magnus, a request for many probabilities can be made faster in two ways---batching and parallelization---that act differently and do not combine.

\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Batching} means passing an array in one call: many energies at one baseline, or many baselines at one energy. (A call can also take an array of energies and an array of baselines of the same length, which it reads as a list of points, the $i$-th energy with the $i$-th baseline. Such a list is not batched: each point is computed on its own. There is no single call that returns the probability at every energy for every one of several baselines; a caller who wants that table makes one call per baseline, each passing the full array of energies, and each of those calls is batched.)

A request with one shared value reaches one of the batched engines of Sec.~\ref{sec:engines}, which reuse work across the array. The \textit{energy-batched scan} evaluates the profile once and carries the energy as a batch dimension through every slab; the \textit{cumulative scan} traverses the profile once and records the running product at every requested baseline. Both run in a single process. The gain is that the profile is not traversed once per point, at no cost in accuracy: where the potential does not vary the batched answers are bit-identical to the point-by-point ones, and where it does they agree to $10^{-12}$ on a shared grid (Sec.~\ref{sec:validation}).
%%%%
\item \emph{Parallelization} means computing points in several processes at once, on several cores, as specified by the {\tt n\_jobs} argument. \magnus\ uses the {\tt joblib} library for it: {\tt n\_jobs} processes are started, the requested points are divided among them, and each point is computed on the per-point path. The work is not reduced, only shared, so the gain is bounded by the number of cores and by the cost of moving work between processes.

The gain is well below the number of parallel processes. Section~\ref{sec:cost} measures it on an Earth chord where every energy has its own baseline, so that no batched engine applies. On a machine with ten cores (Sec.~\ref{sec:timing_protocol}), ten processes complete a scan of $1000$ energies about twice as fast as one process does, a scan of $5000$ energies $2.5$ times faster, and a scan of $20\,000$ energies $2.8$ times faster: the gain grows with the size of the scan and stays far from 10. 
% \ref{app:workers} shows that the exact figure also depends on how it is measured.
\end{itemize}

Batching and parallelization exclude each other. The batched engines answer only at {\tt n\_jobs} $= 1$; any other value sends the scan to the per-point path, computed in parallel, which is slower than the batched path in one process. For a three-flavor scan of $5000$ energies between $0.5$ and $20$~GeV along the PREM chord at $\cos\theta_z = -0.9$, all at the one baseline of $11\,468$~km, the batched path takes $0.11$~s in one process; ten processes take $1.1$~s (Section~\ref{sec:cost}). The rule that follows is simple. Pass arrays, and leave {\tt n\_jobs} at its default of 1. Set {\tt n\_jobs} above one only for a scan that no batched engine accepts, such as one where every energy has its own baseline, which is the case \figu{njobs_scaling} prices.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{Declaring the structure of a profile}
\label{sec:declaring_edges}

Two keywords let the caller place slab edges by hand. They serve similar, but different purposes:
\begin{itemize}
\item {\tt t\_breakpoints} names positions that must be slab edges. The ladder of Section~\ref{sec:refinement} keeps its own grid, inserts an edge at each named position at every refinement level, then refines as usual to the requested tolerance. Use it whenever the profile has a known density jump, a kink, or a front at a known position: the caller states where the profile is not smooth; the package chooses the resolution. 

Two cases in this paper are of this kind. First, a supernova ray crosses a forward shock and a contact discontinuity whose positions the shock model gives (Section~\ref{sec:shock}); declaring the two positions is what lets the refinement resolve a front narrower than any slab. Second, a tabulated density profile, interpolated linearly between its points, has a kink at every point of the table; declaring the points keeps each slab on one linear piece.
%%%%
\item {\tt t\_slab\_edges} gives the complete grid of positions at which the evolution operator is evaluated, as a list of $[{\rm start}, {\rm end}]$ pairs, one per slab. It replaces the grid of the refinement ladder; the slab count is fixed, and the refinement of the slab count is switched off. This should be used only for a grid that can be justified in full, typically with no tolerance requested, so that the probability call is a single evaluation of the expansion on that grid. The caller then takes over the accuracy from the ladder: nothing refines the grid. {\tt t\_breakpoints} is ignored when {\tt t\_slab\_edges} is given.

This keyword is intended for piecewise-constant profiles, such as the castle wall of \figu{arrangement}. With one slab per constant piece the result is exact, because the Hamiltonian is constant within each slab and the Magnus expansion terminates at its first term. For every other profile, {\tt t\_breakpoints} is the keyword to use.
\end{itemize}

The most common use case is that of a neutrino traveling inside the Earth; \magnus\ handles it without the caller's involvement. PREM describes the Earth as concentric layers, with the density varying smoothly inside each layer and jumping at the boundaries between them. The Earth wrappers pass the positions at which a trajectory crosses a boundary through {\tt t\_breakpoints} themselves. On a core-crossing trajectory (zenith angle $\cos\theta_z = -0.9$), which crosses sixteen boundaries, the placement of the slab edges is what makes the order matter. With slabs placed uniformly along the Earth chord, orders 2, 4, and 6 all stall between $10^{-3}$ and $3 \cdot 10^{-5}$ out to two thousand slabs, order 6 no better than order 4. In contrast, with an edge placed at each of the sixteen crossings, the error of every order falls as $h^{p}$ with the slab width, so the three orders differ only in cost: at $5$~GeV, an error of $10^{-8}$ takes about 2000 slabs at order 2, 64 at order 4, and 16 at order 6. Over a set of piecewise-constant test profiles, declaring the jumps lowers the median error by orders of magnitude.

Listing~\ref{lst:edges} shows the use of {\tt t\_breakpoints} and {\tt t\_slab\_edges} on the castle-wall profile of \figu{arrangement}. Declaring the 23 density jumps through {\tt t\_breakpoints} gives the exact answer at the first level at which two stages agree, the grid of the ladder carrying the inserted edges. Giving the 24 slabs through {\tt t\_slab\_edges} gives the same answer in a single evaluation, since the profile is constant within every slab. A grid of twelve slabs, each straddling one jump, is off by $6 \cdot 10^{-4}$; nothing in the package repairs it, because the caller owns that grid. Section~\ref{sec:prem} declares the boundaries of PREM; Sec.~\ref{sec:shock} declares a shock front the same way.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python, caption={The two ways of placing slab edges by hand, on the castle-wall profile of \figu{arrangement}. {\tt t\_breakpoints} adds the positions of the jumps to the grid of the ladder, which then refines as usual; {\tt t\_slab\_edges} replaces the grid with the given [start, end] pairs and fixes the slab count. The first two calls return the exact result, because the profile is constant within every slab. The third call passes a grid whose slabs straddle the jumps: the result is off by $6 \cdot 10^{-4}$, and no refinement corrects it, because the grid is the caller's. Section~\ref{sec:shock} declares the two fronts of a supernova shock through {\tt t\_breakpoints} in the same way.}, label={lst:edges}, float=tbp]
import numpy as np
import magnus.oscprob as oscprob
import magnus.globaldefs as gd

osc = gd.load_nufit_params('NuFIT 6.1')
E, L = 2.0*gd.UNIT_GEV, 6000.0*gd.UNIT_KM
# 24 slabs of 250 km, alternating 2 and 8 g/cm^3
edges = np.linspace(0.0, L, 25)
rho = np.where(np.arange(24) % 2 == 0, 2.0, 8.0)

def castle_wall(l):
    k = np.searchsorted(edges, l, side='right') - 1
    return rho[np.clip(k, 0, 23)]

kw = dict(nu_i=gd.NUMU, nu_f=gd.NUE,
          density_matter_is_in_g_per_cm3=True)
prob = oscprob.osc_prob_matter_std_potential

# The jumps declared: the ladder keeps its own grid,
# adds an edge at each of the 23 jumps, then refines
# to the tolerance
P1 = prob(3, castle_wall, E, L, osc,
          t_breakpoints=edges[1:-1],
          rtol=1.0e-8, atol=1.0e-10, **kw)

# The grid given in full, as [start, end] pairs: one
# slab per layer, no refinement, a single evaluation
pairs = np.column_stack([edges[:-1], edges[1:]])
P2 = prob(3, castle_wall, E, L, osc,
          t_slab_edges=pairs, rtol=None, atol=None,
          **kw)

# A grid of 12 slabs, each straddling one jump: the
# caller owns this grid, and nothing repairs it
wide = np.column_stack([edges[:-1:2], edges[2::2]])
P3 = prob(3, castle_wall, E, L, osc,
          t_slab_edges=wide, rtol=None, atol=None,
          **kw)

# P1 = P2 = 0.0240046, exact: the profile is constant
# within every slab.  P3 = 0.0246158, off by 6e-4
\end{lstlisting}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
