The tolerance a user requests tells the refinement when to stop. It does not guarantee that the answer lies within that tolerance of the truth. Therefore, whether an answer can be trusted has to be read from the warnings. Across the profile families that \magnus\ serves, those warnings fire often on cases that prove to be sound. That is deliberate: most of them report a condition of the input, not a prediction of the error. One failure escapes them entirely. The ladder stops when two successive slab counts agree to within the tolerance. That agreement is evidence of convergence, not proof of it: both levels can be wrong by the same amount and agree anyway. The result is an answer outside the requested tolerance carrying no warning, which Sec.~\ref{sec:validation} calls a silent miss. On randomly generated smooth profiles, the hardest family we tested, about one answer in twenty-five was of that kind.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{Accuracy and speed: smooth density profile}
\label{sec:smooth_comparison}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\begin{figure*}[t!]
 \centering
 \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{smooth_reach.pdf}
 \caption{\label{fig:smooth_reach}Deviation of the computed probability from a reference, $\lvert \Delta P \rvert$, against the time per probability, at two, three, four, and five flavors, on two density profiles. Both \magnus\ and {\tt NuOscProbExact} are scored against the same reference at every point: a {\tt DOP853} integration of the evolution equation at ${\tt rtol} = 10^{-12}$, computed independently at each energy (Sec.~\ref{sec:validation}). {\it Left}: a smooth exponential density profile (as in the Sun). {\it Right}: a core-crossing Earth chord at $\cos\theta_z = -0.9$, along which the PREM profile crosses sixteen layer boundaries; \magnus\ places a slab edge on each of them automatically. The top row shows the two profiles. We dial every curve by a requested tolerance, {\tt rtol}: \magnus\ at orders 4, 6, and 8; {\tt NuOscProbExact}\ \cite{NuOscProbExact}, through its own adaptive refinement. We timed both codes in one process on one machine (Sec.~\ref{sec:timing_protocol}). Both codes and the reference take the electron fraction {\tt NuOscProbExact} uses by default, a uniform $Y_e = 0.5$, in place of \magnus's layered one inside the Earth. The bottom row carries no {\tt NuOscProbExact} curve, since the code stops at four flavors. The full code to reproduce this figure is in notebook \#28 in \Ref\ \cite{Magnus}. See Sec.~\ref{sec:smooth_comparison} for details.}
\end{figure*}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Figure~\ref{fig:smooth_reach} shows the deviation from a converged reference vs.~the time per probability, for \magnus\ and {\tt NuOscProbExact}, at two through five flavors. \magnus\ is called as
\begin{verbatim}
 P = oscprob.osc_prob_matter_std_potential(
    d, ne_of, E, baseline, osc, L0=0.0,
    nu_i=gd.NUMU, nu_f=gd.NUMU,
    density_is_of_number_of_electrons=True,
    magnus_exp_order=4,
    rtol=r, atol=r*1.0e-2)
\end{verbatim}
with {\tt d} the number of flavors, {\tt ne\_of} the electron density along the trajectory and {\tt r} the dial, swept from $10^{-3}$ to $10^{-10}$; orders 6 and 8 differ only in {\tt magnus\_exp\_order}. The left column in \figu{smooth_reach} takes a smooth exponential profile; the right column, an Earth chord through the core, along which the PREM profile crosses sixteen layer boundaries. Every curve is dialed by a requested tolerance. The closed form is the cheaper code wherever it can reach. It stops at an accuracy that no slab count improves on. From there, \magnus\ goes further.

\smallskip

\textit{The floor.}---Composing $N_{\rm slabs}$ constant-density operators accumulates round-off in the product itself. Halving the slab width reduces the discretization error, but it doubles the number of factors in the product and so raises the round-off. Past some tens of thousands of slabs, the round-off added exceeds the discretization error removed, so the total error stops falling. At three flavors that leaves a request for $10^{-10}$ out of reach: the curve flattens an order of magnitude short; a finer cut moves it no further. Section~\ref{sec:shock_comparison} measures the same effect past its turning point, where adding slabs makes the error rise again. This is a property of any long ordered product evaluated in floating point, not a defect in {\tt NuOscProbExact}. \magnus\ meets the same wall two to three decades further down in accuracy, depending on the order, because a higher-order rule reaches a given accuracy in far fewer factors.

\smallskip

\textit{The ceiling.}---Adding flavors limits the two codes differently. The SU($n$) construction---where $n$ is the number of flavors---needs the eigenvalues of the Hamiltonian from its characteristic polynomial, which is soluble in radicals only up to degree four. {\tt NuOscProbExact} implements no five-flavor route, so the bottom row carries no closed-form curve. \magnus\ has no such limit. At four and five flavors its curve is flat between requested tolerances of $10^{-3}$ and $10^{-4}$: both requests return the same answer, already orders of magnitude more accurate than either asks for. From $10^{-6}$ onward each tighter request returns a smaller error, as at two and three flavors.

\smallskip

\textit{Where the crossover falls.}---The closed-form probability in {\tt NuOscProbExact} is the faster code at loose tolerances. Where it stops being faster depends on the profile. Compared at the accuracy each code actually reaches, \magnus\ becomes the cheaper route near a tolerance of $10^{-8}$ on the exponential profile at three flavors, and near $10^{-5}$ on the Earth chord (where the automatically identified PREM discontinuities help the quadrature perform better). The advantage lies in the number of slabs, not in the cost of each. For instance, at two flavors on that chord, a requested tolerance of $10^{-8}$ takes \magnus\ a few hundred slabs against well over a hundred thousand for the closed form.

Still at two flavors, a \magnus\ slab at order 4 costs about four times a closed-form slab: two Gauss-Legendre samples of $\mathbb{A}$, a commutator, the assembly of $\Omega$, and the exponential of a matrix, against one optimized SU(2) expression at one density in {\tt NuOscProbExact}~\cite{Bustamante:2019ggq}. A slab is only a few hundred floating-point operations at these matrix sizes. At that size the overhead of reaching the arithmetic---Python calls, loop dispatch, temporary arrays---can exceed the arithmetic itself. \magnus\ runs each slab in compiled kernels, so the factor of four reflects the arithmetic in the slabs, not that overhead. 

\magnus\ does carry one cost the closed form does not: the refinement evaluates and discards every level below the one that converges. At the loosest requested tolerances that fixed cost alone exceeds what the closed form spends in total, so the closed form is the cheaper code at the coarse end of every panel in \figu{smooth_reach}. Past the crossover, the ordering reverses: \magnus\ is faster by a factor of a few at a tolerance of $10^{-6}$ and by more than an order of magnitude at $10^{-8}$.

\smallskip

\textit{What moves the crossover.}---The number of flavors moves the crossover, too, in \magnus's favor. The cost of a closed-form slab climbs steeply with the number of states: on this chord, the SU(4) expression costs nearly thirty times the SU(2) one, carrying more invariants and a longer chain of products. A \magnus\ slab climbs far less, since all that changes is the size of the matrix exponentiated, from $2 \times 2$ to $4 \times 4$. The crossover therefore moves to looser tolerances as flavors are added. At three flavors it sits about an order of magnitude looser than at two.

\smallskip

\textit{The cost of the Hamiltonian.}---Every curve in \figu{smooth_reach} was measured with a PREM density lookup, about as cheap as a Hamiltonian can be. \magnus\ samples $\mathbb{A}$ at two Gauss-Legendre nodes per slab and the closed form reads one density per slab, so the number of evaluations each code requests follows its slab count. Counting every evaluation, including the refinement levels both codes discard, the closed form asks for some thirty times more at a requested $10^{-6}$ and a hundred times more at $10^{-8}$. A PREM density lookup operation is cheap enough that a hundred times more evaluations add almost nothing to the time, so none of this shows in \figu{smooth_reach}.

It would show for a more expensive Hamiltonian. At a requested tolerance of $10^{-4}$, \magnus\ comes out ahead as soon as one evaluation of $\mathbb{A}$ costs more than a few tens of nanoseconds; from $10^{-6}$ down, it is ahead whatever an evaluation costs. A few tens of nanoseconds is not an exotic price: the tabulated BS2005-AGS,OP solar model of \figu{solar}, interpolated log-linearly at every sample, already crosses it. For a Hamiltonian more expensive than that, the crossover leaves the range of \figu{smooth_reach} altogether.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{Accuracy and speed: a supernova shock front}
\label{sec:shock_comparison}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\begin{figure}[t!]
 \centering
 \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{shock_cost.pdf}
 \caption{\label{fig:shock_cost}Time to compute one probability to within $\lvert \Delta P \rvert \leq 10^{-7}$ on the supernova ray of \figu{shock}, against the width of both of its shock fronts. The upper axis divides each width by the oscillation length at the front, about $16$~km (Sec.~\ref{sec:shock}). For every width, and separately for every curve, we find the smallest slab count that reaches the target and time that setting. Each time is the best of three timed blocks, taken after a discarded warm-up pass, divided by 61---the number of baselines one call returns---so the figure gives the cost of one probability inside a scan rather than the cost of a whole scan. \textit{Front declared} passes the two edges of each front to the probability routine through {\tt t\_breakpoints}; \textit{front resolved} marks points inside the front as well. The full code to reproduce this figure is in notebook \#28 in \Ref\ \cite{Magnus}. See Sec.~\ref{sec:shock_comparison} for details.}
\end{figure}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Figure~\ref{fig:shock_cost} shows the time to compute one probability to an accuracy of $\lvert \Delta P \rvert \leq 10^{-7}$ on the supernova ray of \figu{shock}, against the width of both of its fronts, from $0.07$ to $701$~km, using \magnus\ and {\tt NuOscProbExact}. \magnus\ is called as
\begin{verbatim}
 P = oscprob.osc_prob_matter_std_potential(
    3, ne_shock(width), E, Ls, osc, L0=L0,
    density_is_of_number_of_electrons=True,
    t_breakpoints=front_edges(width, n, resolve),
    magnus_exp_order=order, cumulative=True,
    n_slabs=n, max_n_slabs=n,
    rtol=1.0e-12, atol=1.0e-14)
\end{verbatim}
with {\tt E} the neutrino energy, $15$~MeV, and {\tt Ls} an array of $61$ baselines along the ray, so that one call returns $61$ probabilities. The function {\tt ne\_shock(width)} gives the electron density along the ray of \figu{shock}. Beyond the forward shock, the star is undisturbed and the density falls as $r^{-2.4}$. The shock compresses it about tenfold at the front; behind the front a rarefaction lowers it to about a fifth of the undisturbed value by the contact discontinuity, across which it rises again by a factor of $2.5$. The two fronts stay at $30\,323$ and $12\,348$~km, and {\tt width} sets the distance over which each makes its transition. The function {\tt front\_edges} returns the two edges of each front, together with points inside them when {\tt resolve} is {\tt True}; {\tt t\_breakpoints} forces a slab edge at each of those positions. Giving {\tt n\_slabs} and {\tt max\_n\_slabs} the same value leaves the refinement no room to grow, so each call runs at exactly {\tt n} slabs.  We dial the slab count rather than the tolerance because on this ray a tighter request does not reliably give a better answer here: the error is set by the slab spanning the front, which refining the rest of the ray does not change. We make this call repeatedly at trial values of {\tt n}, take the smallest that reaches the target $\vert \Delta P \vert$, and plot the run time at that value. 

For every width we find, separately for each curve in the figure, the smallest slab count that reaches that accuracy, then time that setting. The width of the front sets the cost, for different reasons for each code. \magnus\ becomes expensive when the front is narrower than the spacing of its slabs and the user marks only its two \textit{declared} edges, which leaves a single slab spanning the front. Marking points inside it as well---\textit{resolving} it---removes the penalty. {\tt NuOscProbExact}---and \magnus\ at order 2, which is equivalent---becomes expensive at intermediate widths, where the density changes too fast for a slab to hold it constant and too slowly for the front to act as a single jump.

\smallskip

\textit{Declaring a front vs.~resolving it.}---Declaring the two edges of a front---via {\tt t\_breakpoints}---puts a slab edge on each of them and brackets the front; these edges stay pinned at every level of the refinement. Everywhere else, the grid stays uniform, with a spacing equal to the ray's length divided by the slab count. On a $70\,000$-km ray, that spacing remains far wider than a $0.07$~km front until the slab count reaches some $250\,000$; so, until then, no uniform point falls inside the front. The front is spanned by a single slab, pinned between the two edges that were declared. Below that count, raising the slab count narrows all the other slabs on the ray while the one spanning the front keeps its width. That one slab then dominates the total error, since it is the only place where the Hamiltonian changes sharply. Narrowing the others does nothing about it. Measured separately (not shown in \figu{shock_cost}), at Magnus order 4, the deviation $\lvert \Delta P \rvert$ sits on a plateau between $2.5 \cdot 10^{-6}$ and $4 \cdot 10^{-6}$, from $10\,000$ to $250,000$ slabs. Resolving the front by marking points inside it, and not only at its edges, removes that plateau. Eight slabs per front are enough to remove it and reach the target of $\lvert \Delta P \rvert \leq 10^{-7}$. Figure~\ref{fig:shock_cost} shows both cases as the \textit{front declared} and \textit{front resolved} curves; we explain their differences next.

\smallskip

\textit{What resolving the front gains in time.}---At $0.07$~km, reaching $\lvert \Delta P \rvert \leq 10^{-7}$ at Magnus order 4---the default order---costs $27$~ms per probability with the fronts declared and $0.5$~ms with them resolved. How much resolving the front saves in run time depends on how many slabs the uniform grid would place inside it by itself. When it would place less than one, resolving the front saves a factor of tens; when it would place a few, a factor of a few; at a 9-km front, where it places nineteen, a factor under two. The order-4 declared and resolved curves cross around 30~km. Past that width, resolving the front only adds work: at 700~km, the grid already places twelve hundred slabs inside the front; the extra edges add $60\%$ to the cost. Thus, one should resolve a front only when the grid would not reach inside it by itself.

\smallskip

\textit{Why a front of middling width is expensive.}---Two curves in \figu{shock_cost} behave unlike \magnus\ at order 4 (and order 6): the cost \magnus\ at order 2, and of {\tt NuOscProbExact}, rises as the front widens, peaks at intermediate widths of 20--50 km, then falls. 

This is explained by what a front does to a neutrino as it crosses it. In matter, the propagating neutrino states are combinations of the flavor states, fixed by the local density. A shock front changes the density, so it rotates those combinations. Across a wide front, the density changes slowly compared with the oscillation length (top axis of \figu{shock_cost}), so the states rotate slowly and the neutrino remains in the state it started in. \Ie, the crossing is adiabatic. In contrast, a thin front spans a small fraction of an oscillation length. The neutrino has no time to adjust, so it crosses unchanged. The density beyond the front is different, so the combinations that propagate there are different too. The neutrino is no longer in one of them; it is in a mixture of them. Both limits are cheap to compute. Across an adiabatic crossing the neutrino stays in one state, so the front never has to be resolved. Across a sudden crossing the whole change sits at one point, where a declared breakpoint puts a slab edge, so the front is represented exactly. A middling front is in neither limit, and there the slabs have to do the work.

The expensive intermediate range of widths can be located in two ways. The first is to measure the front width against the oscillation length, which varies along the ray with the local density. The comparison that matters is at the front itself, where the oscillation length is about $16$~km (Sec.~\ref{sec:shock}). The upper axis of \figu{shock_cost} divides each width by that value. The cost peaks between 2 and 5 oscillation lengths---one way to define what \textit{intermediate width} means. The second way needs no run at all. The non-adiabaticity $\gamma_{jk}$ of \equ{hf} is large when a crossing is sudden and small when it is adiabatic. Evaluated across the front and taken at its largest over pairs of instantaneous eigenstates, it falls from about 730 at the thinnest front to about 0.08 at the widest. It passes through unity between 38 and 62~km, which is where the measured costs peak.

\smallskip

\textit{Why the commutator removes the peak.}---A constant-density slab keeps the Hamiltonian fixed across its length. At a middling front, the state rotation happens inside the slabs, so each is wrong by a little and narrowing them is the only remedy to track the changing Hamiltonian, which yields the peak that \magnus\ at order 2 and {\tt NuOscProbExact} show in \figu{shock_cost}. Magnus at orders 4 and above sample $\mathbb{A}$ at more than one point inside each slab and keep the commutators between those samples. The commutators capture the changing Hamiltonian directly inside a slab, so the changing Hamiltonian is accounted for without having to narrow slabs, decreasing the number of slabs needed and, as a consequence, the run time. Each higher Magnus order keeps more samples and flattens the curve further.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{Accuracy and speed: the Sun}
\label{sec:solar_comparison}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\begin{figure}[t!]
 \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{solar_average_cost.pdf}
 \caption{\label{fig:solar_average_cost}Cost of one averaged survival probability through the Sun, for eight configurations at $5$~MeV. The density profile is the tabulated BS2005-AGS,OP model, log-interpolated, not the exponential fit that {\tt osc\_prob\_*\_sun} assumes; the two are different problems and cost differently. The evolution is adiabatic throughout and every eigenvalue pair has decohered on arrival, so the level-crossing matrix is the identity and \equ{averaged_varying} needs only one eigendecomposition at production and another at detection, exact to about $10^{-15}$. We compare that against the same average obtained by integration: a {\tt DOP853} solve at ${\tt rtol} = 10^{-8}$, the loosest tolerance that still reproduces the closed form, returns the probability oscillating along the ray. Its mean over the last stretch, where the neutrino leaves the Sun, is the quantity wanted. The quoted time covers both steps. The nonzero active-sterile mixing parameters are $\sin^2\theta_{14} = \sin^2\theta_{24} = 0.1$ and $\Delta m^2_{41} = 1$~eV$^2$ for 3+1, and $\sin^2\theta_{15} = \sin^2\theta_{25} = 0.05$ and $\Delta m^2_{51} = 2$~eV$^2$ for 3+2. Their integrations are projected, since eV-scale splittings put some $10^{7}$ oscillations along the ray and resolving them takes tens of hours; we extrapolate from a measured segment of the same ray. The full code to reproduce this figure is in notebook \#28 in \Ref\ \cite{Magnus}. See Sec.~\ref{sec:solar_comparison} for details.}
\end{figure}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Figure~\ref{fig:solar_average_cost} shows what the averaged survival probability of a $5$-MeV solar neutrino, \equ{averaged_varying}, costs for two to five flavors, with and without non-standard interactions and Lorentz violation. \magnus\ is called as
\begin{verbatim}
 P = oscprob.osc_prob_3nu_sun(E,
    gd.SUN_RADIUS*gd.UNIT_KM, 0.0,
    **osc, average=True,
    nu_i=gd.NUE, nu_f=gd.NUE)
\end{verbatim}
with {\tt osc = gd.load\_nufit\_params('NuFIT 6.1')}, for the standard three-flavor case, and similarly for the others. We compare that against the same average obtained from a {\tt DOP853} integration. \magnus\ is four or more orders of magnitude faster. 

The reason is that the integrator has to resolve some $10^{5}$ oscillation cycles along the trajectory before the average discards them. In contrast, \magnus\ never computes an oscillation. It diagonalizes the Hamiltonian at the point of production and at the point of detection, then evaluates \equ{averaged_varying} from the two sets of eigenvectors. What those two diagonalizations cost is set by the dimension of the Hamiltonian, which is the number of flavors, so it is nearly independent of what the Hamiltonian contains: new physics changes the entries of the matrix, not its size.

Almost none of \magnus's time goes into computing the probability. The eigendecompositions are a fraction of a percent of the call. About four fifths goes to the search for non-adiabatic crossings of Sec.~\ref{sec:hybrid}. \magnus\ has to run it because the two-endpoint form holds only if the neutrino stays in the same instantaneous state the whole way out, which a crossing would break. On this profile the search finds none. 
% That search is what justifies the result. With no crossings the neutrino stays in the same instantaneous state the whole way out, so the average depends only on the eigenvectors where it was produced and where it is detected.

\smallskip

% \textit{A general-purpose solver on the same file.}---{\tt nuSQuIDS} needs of order ten minutes on that model file merely to reach a tolerance at which its output is a probability. Averaging the phase away then multiplies that by the number of points the average is taken over. We report this as the cost of asking a general-purpose solver a question it was not built for, not as a defect; the driver is in the repository, so the configuration can be checked and improved. Across three tolerances spanning two decades the cost roughly triples as the output becomes a probability. At the loosest, survival probabilities run from below $-1$ to above $3$. One decade tighter they sit within a percent of $[0,1]$. Only at the tightest does every value fall inside it.

% \textbf{The flavor-sum guard passes on every one of those rows.} Summing the flavor probabilities and checking that they come to one holds to machine precision even where a survival probability approaches $3$. That is not a failure of the guard. {\tt nuSQuIDS} evolves the density matrix in an SU(3) basis whose identity component is the trace, so the flavor sum is conserved by construction, however badly the traceless components are integrated. A structural invariant cannot test the thing it is built into. The check that bites is each probability lying in $[0,1]$.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Comparison to other codes}
\label{sec:comparison}

% We compare \magnus\ against two codes. {\tt NuOscProbExact}\ \cite{NuOscProbExact, Bustamante:2019ggq} appears throughout this section, and in the speed--accuracy planes of Section \ref{sec:performance}. {\tt nuSQuIDS}\ \cite{Delgado:2014kpa, Arguelles:2021twb, NuSQuIDS} appears in Section \ref{sec:solar_comparison} alone, where the observable is the phase-averaged solar probability. We referee every case by a third method that belongs to neither code, and we time every code in one process on one machine. The exception is Section \ref{sec:prem_planes}, whose external timings the companion project measured on the same machine model in a separate session. We keep the datasets behind every figure in the repository, so that none of the external codes need be installed to redraw them.

% \textbf{Where a closed form exists and the accumulated phase is large, use the closed form.} Constant density, piecewise-constant PREM, and standard three-flavor propagation are exactly what closed forms are built for, and on those \magnus\ does not win on cost. What it buys is everything that is not that: accuracy past the point where a piecewise-constant discretization stalls, an arbitrary varying profile, a Hamiltonian nobody has diagonalized, five flavors, and observables we return rather than reconstruct from a scan of instantaneous probabilities.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{Relation to other oscillation codes}
\label{sec:relation}

We compare the performance of \magnus\ against several mature oscillation codes:
\begin{itemize}
\item {\tt GLoBES}\ \cite{GLoBES, Huber:2007ji} is an experiment-simulation framework in which probabilities are one layer inside a package built for fluxes, cross-sections and detector response.
\item {\tt Prob3++}\ \cite{Prob3pp} evaluates the analytic three-flavor probability in matter\ \cite{Barger:1980tf} and is long established in atmospheric analyses.
\item {\tt nuCraft}\ \cite{nuCraft} integrates numerically along a chord through the Earth.
\item {\tt NuFast-LBL}\ \cite{Denton:2024pzc} and {\tt NuFast-Earth}\ \cite{Denton:2025oxf} are fast specializations of the three-flavor case.
\item {\tt NuOscProbExact}\ \cite{NuOscProbExact, Bustamante:2019ggq} evaluates closed-form SU($n$) expansions of the evolution operator for an arbitrary Hermitian Hamiltonian at two, three, and four flavors, and composes them across slabs of constant density.
\item {\tt nuSQuIDS}\ \cite{Delgado:2014kpa, Arguelles:2021twb, NuSQuIDS} is the most general: it propagates a density matrix rather than a state, which admits interactions, attenuation and open-system terms that no closed form reaches, at the cost of solving a differential equation.
\end{itemize}
Structurally, \magnus\ sits between {\tt NuOscProbExact} and {\tt nuSQuIDS}: it composes slab exponentials as the first does, while resolving the variation of the Hamiltonian within each slab as the second would, though via different means.

% Several others serve particular sectors: {\tt OscProb}\ \cite{OscProb} and {\tt CUDAProb3}\ \cite{Kallenborn:2019ilo} evaluate layered Earth profiles for atmospheric analyses, the latter on graphics hardware; {\tt Neurthino.jl}\ \cite{Neurthino} and {\tt NuProbe}\ \cite{Fong:2022oim} extend beyond three flavors; {\tt Nu-Pert}\ \cite{NuPert} expands about a constant density; and {\tt PEANUTS}\ \cite{PEANUTS} computes the solar flux and its composition on an adiabatic treatment, which makes it the natural comparison for the averaged solar observable of Section \ref{sec:sun}. Three of these codes now return derivatives of the probability---analytically in {\tt CHIC}\ \cite{Fernandez-Menendez:2025qbi}, by automatic differentiation in {\tt MANGO}\ \cite{Granger:2026qdr} and {\tt nuTens}\ \cite{nuTens}---which \magnus\ does not, and which a user fitting parameters will want to know before adopting it.

% Structurally, \magnus\ sits between {\tt NuOscProbExact} and {\tt nuSQuIDS}. Against the closed-form code of {\tt NuOscProbExact}, the difference is what happens \emph{inside} a slab: {\tt NuOscProbExact} freezes the Hamiltonian there and is exact for the frozen one, while \magnus\ integrates the Hamiltonian across the slab via the Magnus expansion, and is exact to the order of its truncation. Against {\tt nuSQuIDS}, the difference is generality. A density matrix admits open-system terms that a state vector cannot represent; carrying them means solving a differential equation instead of composing exponentials. \magnus\ does not offer that generality and does not pay for it.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

% \subsection{Where the closed form wins}
% \label{sec:closed_form_wins}

% At constant density both codes are exact, to round-off. Over $60$ energies from $0.6$ to $20$~GeV at $1300$~km through $2.848$~g~cm$^{-3}$, \magnus\ reaches $9.0 \cdot 10^{-17}$ and {\tt NuOscProbExact} $4.9 \cdot 10^{-16}$. Called with the whole energy array at once, \magnus\ costs about seven times more per point. Called one energy at a time, \emph{either} code costs about an order of magnitude more per probability than it does batched. On this problem the dominant effect is how the code is called, not which code it is.

% Through the Earth the two codes point in opposite directions. The closed form is far cheaper, and \magnus\ reaches far deeper. A single figure of merit would hide one of them, so we report the two separately. \figu{smooth_reach} of Section \ref{sec:smooth_comparison} measures both along a core-crossing chord.

% \textit{Cost: the closed form, decisively.}---At three flavors {\tt NuOscProbExact} is cheaper at every setting we can compare. How much cheaper depends on what we ask of each code. Matched at the accuracy the closed form reaches with its densest slabbing, \magnus\ costs a few times more and returns about ten times the accuracy. Compared instead at each code's tightest setting, the ratio grows by about an order of magnitude. Those two settings differ by five orders of magnitude in error, so the second ratio prices reach rather than speed. At $3+1$ the gap is larger and of a different kind. It reaches two orders of magnitude, and the cost does not fall when we loosen the tolerance. That flatness is itself the diagnosis: the refinement ladder is not converging and stopping, but running to its slab ceiling, because an eV-scale $\Delta m^2_{41}$ over an $11\,000$~km chord demands a slab width the ladder never reaches. \magnus\ warns on every such row.

% \textit{Reach: the expansion, decisively.}---Refined against its own tightest setting, \magnus\ reaches $3.3 \cdot 10^{-10}$ at three flavors on that chord. {\tt NuOscProbExact} stalls near $5.6 \cdot 10^{-5}$ at its densest slabbing. The residual between the two codes is $4.1 \cdot 10^{-4}$, the same order as the looser of the two curves, so most of it is the closed form's discretization rather than a disagreement about physics. A residual far above \emph{both} curves would have meant a convention mismatch instead. That is the check we make before we conclude anything from a cross-code difference.

% At $3+1$ the accuracy runs the same way, with a sharper diagnosis. \magnus's residual against the referee is $4.5 \cdot 10^{-8}$, \emph{below} the referee's own discretization error of $4.1 \cdot 10^{-7}$. The honest statement is that the two agree to within the referee's uncertainty, and that this comparison cannot resolve them further. {\tt NuOscProbExact} sits some six hundred times above that floor, and its convergence is \emph{not monotonic}: thirty-two slabs per segment is worse than eight. Non-monotonic convergence is the signature of a discretization whose edges straddle structure they do not resolve. No setting of that dial can be read off as converged, because the error does not fall as we turn it.

% The two findings sit together uncomfortably. \magnus\ warns on every $3+1$ row, correctly, since the answer is not backed by a convergence argument---and it is also the most accurate answer we obtain there. \textbf{A convergence warning is a statement about evidence, not about error}. This is exactly where the two come apart: the warning is correct and the number is accurate at the same time, which no single figure of merit expresses.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{Speed-accuracy comparison}
\label{sec:prem_planes}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\begin{figure*}[t!]
 \centering
 \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{speed_accuracy_combined.pdf}
 \caption{\label{fig:prem_plane}Error in the probability calculation against each code's own reference, plotted against time per probability, for eight codes on three setups: constant density ({\it top}); a core-crossing PREM Earth chord at $\cos\theta_z = -0.9$, baseline $11\,468$~km, at three flavors ({\it middle}); the same chord with one sterile state ({\it bottom}). \magnus\ and {\tt NuOscProbExact} appear twice in the Earth panels because each turns two dials, a slab count and a tolerance; {\tt NuFast-Earth} appears twice because its cost depends on which oscillation parameter the scan varies. See Sec.~\ref{sec:prem_planes} for details.}
\end{figure*}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Figure~\ref{fig:prem_plane} compares the eight codes on three setups: constant density at three flavors, a core-crossing Earth chord at three flavors, and the same chord at 3+1 flavors. Every point is one setting of one code, ranked by the time it takes per probability and the error it returns. Each code is scored against a 50-digit reference built that adopts the code's own constants and conventions--chiefly, the nucleon mass that fixes $V_{\rm CC}$. Thus, two codes can reach $10^{-14}$ on the y-axis of \figu{prem_plane} and still disagree with each other at $10^{-4}$.

Every code evaluates the probability at energies over a fixed logarithmic grid; the error plotted in \figu{prem_plane} is the largest deviation found over that grid. The constant-density panel uses sixty energies from $0.6$ to $20$~GeV; the Earth chord at three flavors, twelve from $3$ to $40$~GeV; the same chord at $3+1$, twelve from $300$ to $30\,000$~GeV, three decades higher because an eV-scale $\Delta m^2_{41}$ puts its resonance there. The mixing parameters are the same for every code: $\delta_{\rm CP} = 217^\circ$, $\Delta m^2_{21} = 7.39 \cdot 10^{-5}$~eV$^2$, $\Delta m^2_{31} = 2.525 \cdot 10^{-3}$~eV$^2$. For the timing, $\delta_{\rm CP}$ is stepped 25 times, with the whole energy grid evaluated at each step.

\smallskip

\textit{Constant density.---}At constant density (\figu{prem_plane}, top panel), the evolution operator is one exponential carrying no discretization at all. Most of the codes land within a factor of a few of double-precision round-off, near $10^{-15}$, and separate only in cost: from $0.04$~$\mu$s for {\tt NuFast-LBL} to about $100$~$\mu$s for {\tt nuSQuIDS}, three orders of magnitude for the same answer. \magnus\ sits at $3 \cdot 10^{-15}$ and $3.9$~$\mu$s. For {\tt GLoBES}, {\tt Prob3++} and {\tt NuOscProbExact}, their refinement dials subdivide a profile that does not vary, so they changes their cost a little and their error not at all; their points lie along a horizontal line.

The second-order expansion has no dial either: it is the analytic formula for $P_{\nu_\mu \to \nu_e}$ to second order in $\alpha = \Delta m^2_{21}/\Delta m^2_{31}$ and $s_{13}$\ \cite{Cervera:2000kp, Freund:2001pn}. It is the second-cheapest point on the panel, but also the least accurate. That error is a truncation of the series rather than a discretization, so no setting moves it.

Only {\tt NuFast} and {\tt nuSQuIDS} trace a curve, the former over the number of Newton steps and the latter over its solver tolerance. The time spread between the two reflects how narrowly each code is specialized to the constant-density problem. {\tt NuFast-LBL} evaluates a three-flavor closed form in constant-density matter as scalar arithmetic, refining its eigenvalues by Newton iteration, which is why it is the cheapest point here. {\tt nuSQuIDS} sits at the other end because it integrates a density matrix through a problem whose answer is a single exponential, paying for generality that constant density does not need.

\smallskip

% \textit{Earth chord, three flavors.---}Through the Earth (\figu{prem_plane}, middle panel), the density changes along the chord, so every code must build its own approximation of the profile. 

\textit{Earth chord, three flavors.---}Through the Earth (\figu{prem_plane}, middle panel) the density changes along the chord, so each code builds its own approximation of the profile, and the figure measures how finely it resolves it.

We show two \magnus\ curves: one varying its slab count, {\tt N\_slabs}, and one varying its tolerance, {\tt rtol}. Given {\tt N\_slabs}, \magnus\ evaluates once at that count: from 4 to 256 slabs, the error falls from $1.3 \cdot 10^{-3}$ to $2.6 \cdot 10^{-10}$, while the cost rises only from about 80 to 430~$\mu$s. Below about thirty slabs, the time is primarily due to overhead; beyond that, the number of slabs drive the time, which doubles with their number. Given {\tt rtol} instead, \magnus\ climbs the refinement ladder and pays for every level it evaluates and discards: ${\tt rtol} = 10^{-8}$ returns $3.2 \cdot 10^{-10}$--the same accuracy as for 256 slabs---for a longer run time of about 1200~$\mu$s. Thus, at matched accuracy, the ladder costs about three times the direct call; this is the price of the convergence evidence it returns with the answer.
% What that buys is reach: the tolerance curve carries on past where the slab sweep ends, reaching even an accuracy of about $4 \cdot 10^{-14}$ at ${\tt rtol} = 10^{-12}$, below the lower edge of the panel.

The other codes reach comparable accuracy at very different cost. To achieve an accuracy of about $3 \cdot 10^{-10}$, {\tt nuSQuIDS} needs 1.3~ms, {\tt NuOscProbExact} 26~ms, {\tt nuCraft} 62~ms, and {\tt GLoBES} 0.5~s, against 0.43~ms for \magnus. The three-flavor specializations are the cheapest codes when little accuracy is asked of them and the most expensive when much is. Their only dial is the number of layers they cut the Earth into, with the cost growing in proportion to that number. \Eg, {\tt NuFast-Earth} starts at 0.6~$\mu$s with an error of 0.18, and reaches $2.5 \cdot 10^{-9}$ only at 34~ms. {\tt nuCraft} runs the other way, starting already at $7 \cdot 10^{-4}$ and never costing less than 4~ms. The difference is where the integrand lives: {\tt nuCraft} hands {\tt SciPy}'s {\tt zvode} function---an ordinary-differential-equation solver--- a right-hand side and Jacobian, which are called once per internal step and rebuild the Hamiltonian each time, so its dial buys accuracy by adding steps that are expensive one by one.

{\tt NuOscProbExact} and \magnus\ cross near an error of $10^{-6}$. Because of its closed-form probabilities, {\tt NuOscProbExact} starts twenty times cheaper: its coarsest setting costs about 4~$\mu$s per probability, against 80~$\mu$s for \magnus. What separates them is the work inside one slab. The closed form samples the density at the slab's midpoint and holds it constant across the slab, which is the second-order Magnus step of Sec.~\ref{sec:order_is_the_slab_product}, so its error falls as the square of the slab width. \magnus\ at order 4 samples the Hamiltonian at two Gauss-Legendre points inside the slab and computes their commutator, so it resolves how the density varies across the slab, and its error falls as the fourth power. The PREM density varies continuously inside every shell, so there is variation for that commutator to resolve. Each doubling of the slab count therefore divides the closed form's error by $2^2$ and \magnus's by $2^4$, which is why the \magnus\ curves eventually cross the {\tt NuOscProbExact} curves, becoming the faster option. 
% Past the crossing, the gap widens: \magnus\ reaches $2.6 \cdot 10^{-10}$ at $0.43$~ms per probability, where {\tt NuOscProbExact} needs $5$~ms due to the growing number of slabs.

Figure~\ref{fig:prem_plane} contains two {\tt NuFast-Earth} curves because it is the only code here whose cost depends on which oscillation parameter a scan moves. Every timed scan in the figure varies $\delta_{\rm CP}$; substituting $\Delta m^2_{31}$ changes the cost of the other codes by at most two per cent. {\tt NuFast-Earth} reuses its layer solve when varying $\delta_{\rm CP}$, so its cost per probability stays near $0.06~\mu$s at every layer count in the sweep. The second {\tt NuFast-Earth} curve is the same sweep of $\Delta m^2_{31}$, a parameter the cache does not cover. Its cost climbs with the layer count and falls inside the band the rest of the panel occupies. 

% {\tt NuFast-Earth}, {\tt GLoBES}, and {\tt Prob3++} descend to between $10^{-7}$ and $10^{-4}$ and stop, held there by the fixed set of shells with which each models the Earth. {\tt NuOscProbExact}, {\tt nuSQuIDS}, and {\tt nuCraft} carry on to a few times $10^{-10}$. \magnus's sweep stops at ${\tt rtol} = 10^{-8}$ because tighter settings leave the panel: on this chord it reaches $3.9 \cdot 10^{-14}$, below the lower edge, and we keep the companion figure's axes unchanged so that the two may be laid side by side. The cost spread is wider than in the top panel, from about a microsecond for {\tt NuFast-Earth} to tens of milliseconds for {\tt nuCraft}.

\smallskip

\textit{Earth chord, $3+1$ flavors.---}The bottom panel of \figu{prem_plane} adds a sterile state, which three of the codes cannot handle: {\tt NuFast-Earth}, {\tt GLoBES} (without extensions), and {\tt Prob3++}. Of the remaining codes, {\tt nuSQuIDS} is the one closer to \magnus.

The crossing between {\tt NuOscProbExact} and \magnus\ survives, moved up an order of magnitude in error to about $10^{-5}$. Each code converges at the rate it did at three flavors: doubling the slab count still divides {\tt NuOscProbExact}'s error by $2^2$ and \magnus's by $2^4$. The head start is what changes: a slab costs {\tt NuOscProbExact} 8 times what it cost at three flavors, against under 3 for \magnus. The reason is the cost of the eigenvalues in {\tt NuOscProbExact}. At three flavors, they come from algebra~\cite{Bustamante:2019ggq}, which is what makes the closed-form probabilities fast. At $3+1$, {\tt NuOscProbExact} must use a more expensive eigensolver instead. Nevertheless, it remains the fastest code at the coarsest accuracies of $10^{-3}$--$10^{-5}$ because it has little overhead.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{When to use \magnus}
\label{sec:when_magnus}

\magnus\ is the code to reach for when the profile, the accuracy, the flavor count, or the Hamiltonian takes a problem outside what a composition of constant-density slabs does well.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{The density varies continuously and fast against the oscillation length.} A composition of constant-density slabs needs some $10^4$ steps per resonance crossing in the Sun, where \magnus\ integrates across the slab instead.
\item \textbf{The profile has structure at a known place.} A shock front, a layer boundary, or a kink is passed as {\tt t\_breakpoints}, which puts a slab edge on it at every refinement level (Sec.~\ref{sec:declaring_edges}).
\item \textbf{The problem has more than three flavors.} At $3+1$ {\tt NuOscProbExact} must find its eigenvalues numerically, at 8 times the cost per slab; past four flavors the SU($n$) closed forms stop altogether.
\item \textbf{The Hamiltonian has no closed form of its own.} Nothing in \magnus\ assumes a form for $\mathbb{H}(l)$ beyond Hermiticity, so a non-standard interaction, a Lorentz-violating background, or a completely novel Hamiltonian is supplied transparently as a matrix and needs no change to the solver.
\item \textbf{The observable is an average.} Phase-averaged probabilities and flavor composition are returned directly instead of reconstructed from a scan (Sec.~\ref{sec:averaged}).
\item \textbf{The accuracy required lies below where a slab composition floors.} On a core-crossing PREM chord, {\tt NuOscProbExact} stops near $2 \cdot 10^{-10}$, while \magnus\ reaches further (Sec.~\ref{sec:prem_planes}). (Both sit far below the per-cent level at which probabilities in data analysis, though.)
\end{itemize}

Elsewhere, another code may be cheaper. On constant density, closed-form and specialized codes reach double precision; the three-flavor specializations {\tt NuFast-LBL} and {\tt NuFast-Earth} do so for about $0.07$~$\mu$s per probability, some sixty times faster than \magnus. On a piecewise-constant profile resolved to no better than about $10^{-6}$, {\tt NuOscProbExact} is the cheapest of them; a three-flavor fit that moves only $\delta_{\rm CP}$ is cheaper still with {\tt NuFast-Earth}. Decay, decoherence, and collective effects lie outside every unitary solver here; they need the density matrix that {\tt nuSQuIDS} propagates (Sec.~\ref{sec:limitations}).

% Each Earth reference is a converged PREM solution rather than an exact one, built by Richardson extrapolation of a high-precision slab product and checked against an adaptive integration through the continuous profile, which shares none of that machinery. The timings for the other codes are the companion project's, frozen in this repository so that none of them need be installed to redraw the figure, and taken in a session of their own on the processor model of Section \ref{sec:timing_protocol}. A cross-code cost carries that caveat; an accuracy does not.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

% \subsection{Making a cross-code number mean anything}
% \label{sec:the_floor}

% \textbf{Check that two codes agree in vacuum before comparing them in matter.} If they disagree in vacuum, the disagreement is in the solvers. If they agree in vacuum and disagree in matter, it is in the conventions---a matter-potential factor, an electron fraction, a channel index---and no tolerance will close it. The signature is a trade-off curve that does not respond to its own dial.

% That is what \magnus\ and {\tt NuOscProbExact} produce against each other's references through the Earth. \magnus's charged-current potential sits $1.9 \cdot 10^{-4}$ below {\tt NuOscProbExact}'s at the same density, the two rounding the nucleon mass differently, and that difference pins \magnus's error against the other code's reference at $2.4 \cdot 10^{-4}$ however tight the tolerance.

% Matching the two potentials does not repair it. Through \magnus's Earth entry points the only handle is {\tt electron\_fraction}, and $V_{\rm CC}$ is not linear in it: \magnus\ derives the average nucleon mass from the composition through $r = n_n/n_p = (1 - Y_e)/Y_e$, so raising $Y_e$ moves the nucleon mass as well and the potential picks up a further $1.3 \cdot 10^{-7}$ that was never asked for. Matching a potential through a composition parameter is only ever good to the order at which composition enters the nucleon mass.

% The repair is a different measurement rather than a better scale factor. We give each code its own high-precision reference, built with that code's own constants, and score it where its conventions are self-consistent. Measured that way on the same chord, \magnus\ reaches $3.9 \cdot 10^{-14}$, four orders below where the matched curve stalled. A code scored against its own reference is being asked how far its own discretization has converged, not how close it sits to another code's answer: two codes can both reach $10^{-14}$ in \figu{prem_plane} and still differ from each other at $10^{-4}$.

% \textbf{A cross-code comparison through the Earth is limited by the agreement of the two Earth models long before it is limited by either solver.} Any such comparison reaching past $10^{-7}$ without saying how its potentials were matched is reporting a convention.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

% \subsection{Choosing between \magnus\ and {\tt NuOscProbExact}}
% \label{sec:which_code}

% \tabl{which} says what to run on eleven problems, comparing \magnus\ with {\tt NuOscProbExact} because the two are close relatives: they take the same arbitrary Hermitian Hamiltonian, compose the same ordered product of slab operators, and share units, conventions, and parameter defaults deliberately. \textbf{A problem is handed to one or the other by the shape of its density profile, and almost never by what its Hamiltonian contains.}

% \textbf{Reach for the closed form unless the problem takes it outside its range.} Smooth variation alone does not: on the exponential profile of \figu{smooth_reach} the closed form is the cheaper code wherever an error near $10^{-8}$ will do. What does take it outside is structure the slab approximation has to chase, such as a layered Earth or a shock front; an accuracy past about $10^{-10}$, where composing that many factors floors it; more than four flavors, where no SU($n$) expression exists; or a Hamiltonian that does not factorize as a scalar potential times a fixed projector.

% Two rows of \tabl{which} resist that sorting. A supernova shock appears twice with opposite answers, separated not by physics but by the width of the front measured against the oscillation length (Section \ref{sec:shock_comparison}). A tabulated profile belongs to \magnus\ because the interpolant between its samples is smooth, so a method that integrates across a slab has something to integrate.

% Because the two packages share conventions, a result that matters can be run both ways; agreement between two methods whose failure modes differ is better evidence than either one's convergence check.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Conclusions}
\label{sec:conclusions}

We have presented \magnus\ \cite{Magnus}, a code that returns neutrino oscillation probabilities for any Hermitian Hamiltonian---standard or not---at any number of flavors, through any density profile. It does so by computing the neutrino evolution operator with the Magnus expansion, a method that expands the operator---which ordinarily requires an integral---in evaluations of the Hamiltonian at different points along the neutrino trajectory, and of the commutators thereof. The size of the Hamiltonian, its contents, and the reason it varies along the trajectory are all free, so a changing matter profile, a Lorentz-violating background, and a sterile neutrino sector are the same calculation for \magnus. For convenience, \magnus\ ships wrappers for two to five flavors across common environments and scenarios.

\magnus\ cuts the trajectory into slabs and computes the evolution operator across each. It then composes the slab operators in the order the neutrino meets them, to evaluate the propagation across the full trajectory. The number of slabs required follows from the tolerance requested of the code. The Magnus expansion ensures the evolution is unitary---\ie, the sum of probabilities between all flavors is one---at any number of slabs and any order of the Magnus expansion. \magnus\ admits pinning edges to  discontinuities along a profile, as it happens inside the Earth or across a supernova shock front.

Along a neutrino trajectory, \magnus\ automatically detects stretches of adiabaticity---where the Hamiltonian varies smoothly and relatively slowly---and of non-adiabaticity---where the Hamiltonian varies more suddenly and its eigenvalues cross---and adapts accordingly. It combines the use of a precise eigensolver for the adiabatic stretches and the Magnus expansion for the non-adiabatic ones.

Further, \magnus\ returns phase-averaged, non-oscillatory probabilities, which describe, \eg, high-energy astrophysical neutrinos that propagate over cosmological-scale baselines.

We have provided multiple examples, each with the code that produces it: layered matter, a core-crossing Earth chord, the Sun, a supernova shock front, five flavors with new physics, and a user-provided Hamiltonian. Each is a variation on one call, with the Hamiltonian, the profile, or the observable changed. A console script computes the same probabilities on a terminal window, without writing any Python code. Multiple tutorial notebooks in the \magnus\ repository~\cite{Magnus} carry each topic further. 

\magnus\ is built for speed without sacrificing accuracy. A scan over neutrino energy or arrival direction is a single batched call to \magnus\ rather than thousands of separate ones. Batched calls are made efficient by using compiled {\tt Numba} kernels. 

Two classes of problem lie beyond \magnus. First, decoherence, coupling to a bath, and decay to invisible states are not unitary and need a density-matrix treatment. Second, collective oscillations in a dense neutrino gas are nonlinear: neutrino self-interaction makes the Hamiltonian change as neutrinos evolve.

\magnus\ is available to install from the Python Package Index with {\tt pip install magnuspy} and from its repository\ \cite{Magnus}.

% ---

% Three properties recommend the method. Each is measured rather than asserted.

% \smallskip
% \textit{Unitary by construction.---}Every truncation of the series is anti-Hermitian, so its exponential lies in the unitary group at any order, any tolerance, and any slab count. Probabilities are non-negative and their rows sum to one to round-off. No accuracy setting moves that defect, which comes from the floating-point exponential alone: $4 \cdot 10^{-16}$ for a single exponential in the median, and $1.6 \cdot 10^{-11}$ at worst for a probability composed of thousands of them.

% \smallskip
% \textit{Indifferent to the phase.---}A slab is exact for a constant Hamiltonian however many oscillation cycles it spans, so the slab width follows how fast the profile changes and not how fast the phase winds. On an exponential profile, at a request of $10^{-8}$ held fixed for both codes, a $160$-fold rise in accumulated phase costs a {\tt DOP853} integration a factor of $54$ in time and \magnus\ a factor of $4$. The two begin a factor of two apart and end a factor of twenty apart, with \magnus\ returning the more accurate answer throughout.

% \smallskip
% \textit{Indifferent to the physics.---}The Hamiltonian is a callable returning a Hermitian matrix of any size. Five flavors, non-standard interactions, a Lorentz-violating background, and a Hamiltonian nobody has written down reach the same solver through the same call.

% \smallskip
% Those properties become three capabilities. Where the eigenvalues of $\mathbb{H}$ cannot be written in radicals, \magnus\ still runs: {\tt NuOscProbExact} stops at four flavors, \magnus\ reaches five. Where a profile carries a feature narrower than the grid, a breakpoint pins a slab edge to it, which on a supernova shock front $0.07$~km wide lowers the cost of one probability from $27$ to $0.5$~ms. Where the observable is the phase-averaged probability, \magnus\ returns it from two diagonalizations instead of resolving the $10^{5}$ oscillation cycles an integration must cross first, four or more orders of magnitude faster. A three-flavor probability through the Earth costs about $2$~ms at the default tolerance, the median over $164$ Earth and solar configurations.

% We are equally clear about where \magnus\ should not be used. Open systems lie outside the method: decay to invisible states, decoherence, and collective effects need a density matrix, which no enlargement of the flavor basis provides. On constant density, and on a piecewise-constant profile resolved to no better than about $10^{-6}$, a closed form is the cheaper code. The requested tolerances are a stopping criterion and not an error bound. On randomly generated smooth profiles, the hardest family we tested, about one answer in twenty-five falls outside the request carrying no warning.

% Two directions remain open. Magnetic resonance has spent sixty years choosing pulse sequences so that whole orders of this same expansion cancel by construction. A matter profile is given rather than chosen, but the composition is ours to design: where the slab edges fall, and how each slab is quadratured. The second direction is composition itself. What a single evaluation costs in accuracy is understood; what a thousand of them chained along a trajectory do to it is not. The integrator literature bounds the discretization error instead of answering that. A code that composes thousands of slabs, tracks its own unitarity, and carries an arbitrary-precision reference is the natural place to settle it.

% \magnus\ is available from the Python Package Index with {\tt pip install magnuspy} and from its repository\ \cite{Magnus}. The repository carries $1250$ tests on Python $3.10$ through $3.13$, covering $93\%$ of the code, and the notebooks of \ref{app:notebooks} that reproduce every figure in this paper.

% \section{Conclusions}
% \label{sec:conclusions}

% We have provided \magnus\ \cite{Magnus}, which computes neutrino oscillation probabilities by propagating the evolution operator with the Magnus expansion, for an arbitrary Hermitian Hamiltonian at two, three, four, or five flavors, through a matter profile that may vary continuously along the trajectory. Neither the expansion nor the collocation integrators built on it are ours; what we provide is their implementation for this problem, together with the slab machinery, the adaptive refinement, the exactly unitary exponential, the composition with adiabatic transport, the closed-form decohered limit, and the batching that makes a whole scan one call.

% Three properties recommend it, each measured rather than asserted. It is \emph{unitary by construction}: every truncation of the series lies in the Lie algebra, so probabilities are non-negative and their rows sum to one to round-off at any accuracy setting, a defect that no accuracy dial moves. The defect does not grow with the baseline, the order, or the tolerance. It is \emph{indifferent to the phase}: a slab is exact for a constant Hamiltonian however many cycles it spans, so what sets the discretization is the profile and not the phase. Measured, the cost grows as $\Phi^{0.21}$ against $\Phi^{0.77}$ for a differential-equation solver asked for the same tolerance on the same problem, and \magnus\ is the more accurate of the two there by nearly three orders of magnitude. It is also \emph{indifferent to the physics}: the Hamiltonian is a callable returning a Hermitian matrix, so five flavors, non-standard interactions, a Lorentz-violating background, and a term nobody has written down are the same call.

% We are equally clear about where it should not be used. It does not treat open systems: decay, decoherence, and collective effects need a density matrix, which no enlargement of the flavor basis provides. It is not the quickest route to a probability in constant or piecewise-constant matter, where a closed form is cheaper by a factor of a few at matched accuracy on a three-flavor Earth chord, and by some $440\times$ at $3+1$. Its tolerances are a stopping criterion rather than an error bound (the same caveat that every adaptive solver carries; Section \ref{sec:refinement} states it in full).

% Two directions remain open. The first is borrowed: magnetic resonance has spent sixty years learning to choose a pulse sequence so that whole orders of this same expansion cancel by construction. The neutrino problem offers the same freedom in a different variable. A matter profile is given rather than controlled, but the composition is ours to design---where the slab edges fall, and how each slab is quadratured---and Section \ref{sec:refinement} shows how much that choice already decides. 

% The second is a question about composition itself. What a single evaluation costs in accuracy is understood; what a thousand of them chained along a trajectory do to it is not. Whether that error grows with the number of slabs, wanders, or largely cancels is not something the integrator literature answers, since it bounds the discretization error instead. A code that composes thousands of slabs, tracks its own unitarity and carries an arbitrary-precision reference is the natural place to settle it.

% None of that touches the case the code was built for: a profile that varies continuously along the path, a Hamiltonian for which no closed form exists, more flavors than a closed form can express, or an observable---the phase-averaged probability of \equ{averaged_varying}---that is better computed than reconstructed. \magnus\ is available from PyPI with {\tt pip install magnuspy}, and from its repository\ \cite{Magnus}.

\section{Magnus expansion at any order}
\label{app:terms}
\appresetfloats

Section \ref{sec:expansion} gave the definition of the Magnus expansion via recursion.  This appendix gives its structure, which is what makes a relatively succinct implementation. The maximum expansion order in \magnus\ is set to {\tt MAGNUS\_EXP\_ORDER\_MAX=10}. Orders nine and ten of the  expansion need either Simpson's rule or the trapezoidal rule to integrate, since the Gauss-Legendre collocation of \equ{gl} stop at order eight ({\tt MAGNUS\_EXP\_ORDER\_MAX\_GL=8}).

\subsection{Computing Magnus terms}

Unrolling the recursion equation, \equ{recursion}, every term of $\Omega_n$ is a right-nested chain
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:nested}
 \left[\Omega_{m_1}, \left[\Omega_{m_2}, \ldots
 \left[\Omega_{m_j}, \mathbb{A}\right] \ldots \right]\right] \;,
 \quad
 \sum_{i=1}^{j} m_i = n-1 \;,
\end{equation}
carrying the coefficient $B_j/j!$. Therefore, the terms of the $j$-th group are indexed by the compositions of $n-1$ into $j$ positive parts, of which there are $\binom{n-2}{j-1}$. Since $B_j$ vanishes for odd $j \geq 3$, the number of terms in $\Omega_n$ is
\begin{equation}
 \label{equ:term_count}
 \#\,\Omega_n = \sum_{j\,:\,B_j \neq 0} \binom{n-2}{j-1} \;.
\end{equation}
The surviving coefficients, of which an implementation to order 10 needs exactly these five, are
\begin{eqnarray}
 \label{equ:bernoulli}
 && \frac{B_1}{1!} = -\frac{1}{2} , \;
 \frac{B_2}{2!} = \frac{1}{12} , \;
 \frac{B_4}{4!} = -\frac{1}{720} , \; \nonumber \\
 && \frac{B_6}{6!} = \frac{1}{30240} , \;
 \frac{B_8}{8!} = -\frac{1}{1\,209\,600} \;.
\end{eqnarray}
A term with $j$ nested commutators requires $j \leq n-1$, so $B_8$ first appears at order 9 (and $B_{10}$ not until order 11). Equation (\ref{equ:term_count}) gives $1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 9, 17, 33, 65, 129$ terms at orders 1 to 10, the count doubling from order 5 onward, which is why \magnus\ implements an order ceiling: the terms remain easy to generate far beyond it, but the work per slab grows with their number.

The module {\tt magnus.expansionterms} evaluates all of this in exact rational arithmetic at any order, with coefficients returned as fractions so that they can be compared exactly, not at a tolerance. This module shares no code with the numerical core of \magnus: the test suite regenerates the hard-coded group factors and compares them against the derived ones, and separately evaluates the generated terms numerically on a sampled $\mathbb{A}(l)$ and compares them order by order against what the core produces internally. Agreement is at machine precision for every order from one to ten, covering the hard-coded low orders and the generated high ones.

\subsection{Why the collocation orders are even}

Two facts fix the leading power of $h$ in each $\Omega_k$. The first is a parity: traversing a slab backwards inverts the evolution, so $\Omega$ changes sign; it does so term by term, since reversal cannot change how many factors of $\mathbb{A}$ a term carries. Measured from the slab midpoint, traversing backwards replaces $h$ by $-h$, so each $\Omega_k$ carries only odd powers of $h$. The second is a cost: above $\Omega_1$, every term is a nested commutator of $\mathbb{A}$ with itself at different positions, which vanishes for constant $\mathbb{A}$. The second fact is the effect of the commutators. Above $\Omega_1$, every term holds at least one commutator of $\mathbb{A}$ at two points of the slab. That commutator vanishes for constant $\mathbb{A}$, so its magnitude follows the variation of $\mathbb{A}$ across the slab, itself $\mathcal{O}(h)$ for smooth $\mathbb{A}$. Each term above $\Omega_1$ thus gains one power of $h$ beyond its $k$ integrations, placing $\Omega_k$ at $h^{k+1}$.

The two facts meet at odd $k$, where $h^{k+1}$ is an even power. The parity forbids it, so the term enters at $h^{k+2}$ instead. Hence $\Omega_1$ enters at $h$ and $\Omega_2$ at $h^3$; from $\Omega_3$ upward the terms pair off, $\Omega_{2j-1}$ and $\Omega_{2j}$ both entering at $h^{2j+1}$ for $j \geq 2$. Both statements of Sec.~\ref{sec:quadrature} follow. A scheme of order $p$ carries a local error $\mathcal{O}(h^{p+1})$, so it retains every term entering below that power: $\Omega_1$ alone at $p = 2$, $\Omega_1$ through $\Omega_{p-2}$ for $p \geq 4$. The leading term it drops sets its error on one slab, at an odd power of $h$. A trajectory of $N$ slabs accumulates those errors, so $h^q$ on each leaves $h^{q-1}$ over the whole path. Odd $q$ makes $q-1$ even: the schemes exist at even order only.

% \section{What a parallel speed-up depends on}
% \label{app:workers}
% \appresetfloats

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% \begin{figure*}[t!]
%  \centering
%  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{njobs_protocol.pdf}
%  \caption{\label{fig:njobs_protocol}The speed-up of \figu{njobs_scaling}, on the two scans too short to time reliably on this machine, each measured three ways. {\it Left}: $500$ energies, a scan that runs in $0.4$~s in one process. {\it Right}: $1000$ energies, $0.7$~s. {\it Grey}: the worker counts swept in a fixed order, which is how the two larger scans of \figu{njobs_scaling} were taken. {\it Blue}: the order redrawn at random in every round. {\it Purple}: the order redrawn at random and every worker pinned to the eight efficiency cores, so that all of them run on identical hardware; that arm therefore stops at eight. Every point is the mean over $30$ interleaved rounds. The grey curve leaves the left-hand panel below $1$, which is where it is worst and is the point of the figure.}
% \end{figure*}
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

% Figure~\ref{fig:njobs_protocol} shows the speed-up of the two shortest scans of \figu{njobs_scaling}, each measured three ways. A 500-energy scan runs in 0.4~s and a 1000-energy one in 0.7~s, so the cost of the measurement itself is a visible fraction of the run time. At these two sizes, the timing protocol changes the answer by as much as the scan size does.

% \smallskip

% \textit{The order matters.}---Sweeping the worker counts in a fixed order biases them, because the first count in a round meets a machine in a different state from the last. At 1000 energies, that protocol returned $2.23\times$ at ten workers against $1.87\times$ once the order was redrawn at random in every round, 19\% high. Randomizing removes the bias; it does not remove the roughness.

% \smallskip

% \textit{Which cores matters.}---The machine used for timing has ten physical cores: eight efficiency cores and two faster performance ones. Pinning every worker to the eight efficiency cores gives all of them identical hardware. The curve that results is smoother and lower: part of the scatter in the unpinned arms is which cores the scheduler happened to pick, and part of the gain in them is the two performance cores. Eight identical cores reach $1.7\times$ at 1000 energies, far short of the eightfold gain that many workers would give if the work divided perfectly. Therefore, the mixture of core types explains only part of that gap.

% \smallskip

% \textit{One effect we cannot explain.}---Even and odd worker counts give speed-ups that differ by about 20\%. That alternation reproduced in three separate runs at 1000 energies. It is visible along the 1000-energy curve of \figu{njobs_scaling}. We tested three candidates: how the energies divide among the tasks, how many energies each worker receives, and the number of workers itself. None explains the alternation we see.

% \smallskip

% \textit{Three sample sizes not measured alike.}---Only the 1000-energy arm of \figu{njobs_scaling} used the randomized order. Its 5000- and 20\,000-energy arms used the fixed one, the protocol that reads 19\% high at 1000 energies. We did not measure that bias at the two larger sizes; their scatter is smaller there, but scatter bounds the roughness, not the bias. The $2.5\times$ and $2.8\times$ reported there may therefore be high by a comparable margin. The growth of the speed-up with scan size is a trend, not three numbers on one footing.

% The spread we quote is stricter than the one Section \ref{sec:timing_protocol} uses. There the coefficient of variation is taken across blocks run back to back; here it is taken across rounds separated in time, always the larger of the two. On the arm \figu{njobs_scaling} plots it is 0.13 at 1000 energies, 0.023 at 5000, and 0.017 at 20\,000; across the three protocols drawn here it reaches 0.20 at 1000 energies and 0.38 at 500. That is why \figu{njobs_scaling} stops at 1000 energies. No number in this appendix should be expected to repeat on another machine.

\section{The test suite}
\label{app:tests}
\appresetfloats

The repository carries about 1400 tests covering 92\% of the library, run on Python 3.10 through 3.13 on every push and every pull request. Coverage is a gate rather than a report: the suite fails below 90\%. A release runs the same suite again, from a built wheel, before anything reaches the index. The tests are not packaged with the library, but are obtainable from a clone,
\begin{verbatim}
pip install -e ".[test]"
pytest
\end{verbatim}

The test suite is not only a regression net. It checks five different kinds of thing.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The mathematics, vs.~an external implementation.} The Magnus recursion coded independently, closed-form vacuum and constant-density expressions, a tight {\tt DOP853} integration against probabilities computed by \magnus, an exact {\tt expm} where the profile makes it exact.
%%%%
\smallskip
%%%%
\item \emph{The properties a probability must have.} Unitarity, the behavior of reversed channels, agreement between the scalar, vectorized, and compiled paths.
%%%%
\smallskip
%%%%
\item \emph{Identities rather than tolerances.} The batched scan against the per-point path, a parallel run against a serial one, so that an optimization which changed an answer fails rather than passes quietly.
%%%%
\smallskip
%%%%
\item \emph{The distribution of the error over random profiles}, rather than individual cases.
%%%%
\smallskip
%%%%
\item \emph{The repository against its own documentation.} The documented file tree is generated from a table and checked against what is tracked, the notebooks are checked against the generator that writes them, and the notebooks' README is checked against the notebooks it describes. Adding a file fails the suite until the table follows it.

\end{enumerate}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The tutorial notebooks}
\label{app:notebooks}
\appresetfloats

The {\tt notebooks/} folder contain 29 notebooks, numbered in suggested reading order. They are the long form of several of the examples in Sec.~\ref{sec:examples}: what is a paragraph and a figure here is, in a notebook, the same calculation with the reasoning around it---why a convention is what it is, what happens at the edges of the parameter range, and what the numbers were checked against. Each is stored with its outputs, so it can be read without being run. Each ends with a pointer to its neighbors. The figures that cost hours to produce are cached in the repository as well: with {\tt MAGNUS\_PAPER\_CACHE\_ONLY} set, as continuous integration sets it, notebook \#28 redraws them from stored data rather than recomputing them, and fails rather than silently recomputing if a cache entry is missing.

The notebooks are generated: one script builds them, executes them, and refuses to finish if any came back without stored outputs. Because a notebook is documentation that claims to work, continuous integration executes them to ensure this. It does so on one interpreter, splitting the load of the 29 notebooks across four parallel shards. It keys a cache on the package source and on the notebook inputs: a notebook whose inputs have not moved is served from that cache rather than run again, to save time. Therefore, a change to the package re-executes all of the notebooks, and a change to one notebook re-executes that one alone. The unit tests run across the four supported versions of Python; the notebooks are not.

\tabl{notebooks} lists the notebooks. Three notebooks are especially important. Notebook \#28 produces every figure in the paper: the probabilities are computed as the notebook runs, except in the figures comparing this code against another, where every code's numbers---including this one's, where the timing has to be comparable---are read from data frozen in the repository. Notebook \#25 is the whole of Secs.~\ref{sec:performance} and \ref{sec:comparison}: every comparison against another code is made and discussed there. Notebook \#13 is the tabulated solar model of Sec.~\ref{sec:sun}, including the window-mean sweep that drifts further from the answer the wider the window is.
